# Server Setup

*How a rented machine is made able to render and how that act is detected, ordered, recorded, interrupted, resumed and safely run again; how Blender builds are listed, fetched, sent up, unpacked, found, matched and removed; how far the add-on offer's machinery reaches and what is read back after an install; how the Telegram role's material is placed by either of its two entry points; how the machine is restarted as part of a setup step and what stands while it is down; how elevation is obtained, how a permission refusal is told apart from every other failure, and what the user-facing fallback re-run does on a machine whose supervisor is dead.*

*2026-10-27 — Removed the withdrawn paid-lock dependency under the Creator’s free-and-open-source decision of 2026-10-04. No auditor has seen this removal.*

Rule 1's plain-English rule is suspended here, per the Architecture stage's exception.

**Scope, from [The shape](The%20shape.md#11-server-setup):** readiness and runtime-health inspection; Blender and add-on installation and removal; Telegram worker installation; user-facing fallback re-run for supervisor-independent package repair and replacement, going through the same package owner and the same trusted release selection as every automatic path; restart; resumable steps; exclusion; privilege escalation; stop; and safe re-run. **§11 was made to agree at the Architecture seam close; [Section 13](#13-unresolved) item 20 records the seam and its closure.** This document needs `Servers`, `Rented-server runtime`, `Server protocol and transfers`, `Security and licence` and `State, identity and recovery`; supplies installed Blenders and the Telegram worker to `Render Settings`, `Dependency Upload & Remap`, `Rendering` and `Telegram`; reads live-job use from `Queue & History`; and `Telegram` invokes its independent machine-role installation entry point.

Rows 527 and 528 were added to the table below on 2026-10-11 and this count did not move with them; Row 526 was never added to the table and is added on 2026-10-16.)* *(Rows 355 to 365 were answered on 2026-08-21, Rows 439, 440 and 443 on 2026-08-23 and Rows 487 to 490 on 2026-08-25; Y6a, W3a, T6a, B8a, B6a, B12a, Y8a, Y8b, M1a, M1b, Y4a, Y6b, S2a and B7a are written for them, and Rows 358, 360, 361, 364 and 365 are answered by clauses that already stood.)* Row 102 is struck and is not answered.

## What binds this before anything is decided

**Fact 51 is answered yes and `Method`'s oldest escalation is spent.** `systemctl reboot` issued over the connection Smoozy already held; the connection reported `closed by remote host`; the same address and port answered again in about ten seconds with a new boot id and the disk intact ([Facts](Facts.md), Fact 51, 2026-08-13). No stop is opened on it and no row below treats it as open. What is left is a narrowing: Fact 27's ready-action weight shows the restart refused outright to an ordinary login — `Failed to reboot system via logind: Interactive authentication required.` — because an SSH session is not a local seat. **The restart is an elevated act**, and [Section 8](#8-the-restart) says which account performs it.

**Fact 50 is answered NO against a stub with no guard, and Row 114's promise is kept by construction rather than by care.** A second ready action on a half-finished machine was not refused; it started its own worker while the machine's state named only the first run, walked all five boundaries from the beginning, and cleared the machine-level state itself — a machine with two of five steps genuinely done marked ready by a run nobody authorised ([The last two boxes](The%20last%20two%20boxes%20—%20the%20hook%20that%20cannot%20be%20read%20back,%20the%20full%20disk,%20and%20the%20unit%20that%20kills%20a%20render.md) §16). **The mechanism that closes it is two clauses and they close different halves of it — named separately 2026-08-29 at the stage-close gate, which found this paragraph giving the whole of it to P8.** The second submission never starts: `Rented-server runtime` **A5b** refuses it at the admission point rather than queueing it, naming the operation standing in its way, and A11a admits one worker start and only the one the standing state names. What a run that **did** start does with a state it does not own is §9 **P8**'s — a run that finds the state set and naming a different run does not clear it, reports it, and what happens next is this document's. **The stub failed at both halves** — not refused, and clearing a state it did not own — so both are named; and [Section 4](#4-interruption-resumption-and-the-safe-re-run) is what happens next. *(P8's own actor is a run P2 and A11a never admit; that sentence is the owning document's and is not repaired from here.)*

**On a container rental nothing is open at all.** Root inside holds 13 of 41 capabilities; all four routes to the driver are closed; **a restart is not a recovery action there** — across two container restarts the host's uptime rose monotonically, so the host never rebooted and the driver was never reloaded — and **a card that has stopped answering is terminal for that server** ([Six machines](Six%20machines%20—%20the%20container,%20the%20webhook%20and%20the%20ready%20action.md), Fact 48). From a non-root login, elevation demands a password the login does not have and each of the three things a ready action needs is refused (Fact 27, ready-action weight). **So no single ready action is written here; [Section 2](#2-the-ready-action) is a detection step and two branches**, and one branch's honest answer is *it already is ready, or it cannot be made so*. **Narrowed 2026-08-23; this used to open *A container rental is the norm*, which is one seller's claim about its own instances stated as a property of rentals.** Fact 48's *"Instances are almost always Docker containers; a small subset are virtual machines"* is **Vast.ai's own documentation about Vast.ai**, read 2026-08-15, and the three-seller batch of 2026-08-22 delivered **virtual machines on the other two sellers** ([The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20%E2%80%94%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md)). **Nothing here turns on which kind is commoner**: every rule in this document is written per kind ([M2](#1-the-machine-kind-and-what-a-setup-can-be-on-each)) off a reading taken at every placement and never carried forward (K2). **The container observations above are that rental's own and do not move.**

**Fact 47's spread is the finding and the mechanism is what a ready action is built on.** Four machines needed nothing, two things, four things and seven things — a property of the image, not of rented machines. All four agree: the blocker is the X11/OpenGL shared-library chain, `libgl1` is needed even for a background render because the executable links `libGL.so.1` at load time, and **Blender names only the first missing library — the whole list is read at once, from `ldd`, never from the launch error.** One trap: a driver metapackage incidentally supplied six of nine missing libraries on one box, so **the list is installed explicitly and never left to the driver's dependencies** ([Six machines](Six%20machines%20—%20the%20container,%20the%20webhook%20and%20the%20ready%20action.md), Fact 47).

**Fact 80 gives a rule and takes away a signal.** Ubuntu's reboot-required flag was never set on the machine that needed a restart most. The rule that survives both observed mechanisms: **run `nvidia-smi` after installing and restart only if it fails** — never unconditionally, never on the package manager's word. Avoiding the restart by module unload and reload left the machine oscillating between working and inconsistent, and **no reading taken while a machine is in that state is acted on here, card detection included** (Fact 80). **Narrowed 2026-08-23 to what the register carries.** This clause used to read *no reading taken in that state can be trusted, card detection included*, and Fact 80's correction of 2026-08-20 says that flat form is written as a permanent property of what is an ordinary repairable state: it names the repair NVIDIA does document — `nvidia-modprobe` creates the character device files, and only *"when … the NVIDIA character device files do not yet exist"*, so a stale file that does exist is not replaced — and records that **that repair is undocumented in operation and unposed** by this project. **Nothing below moves**, because nothing here ever enters the state or repairs it: [R5](#22-what-the-action-never-does) forbids the module unload and reload outright, and [R1](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of) item 6's restart is the only route out of a failed check. Restart cost varied **9 to 90 seconds** across eight measurements on three machines, so **nothing here budgets a fixed wait** (`Rented-server runtime` K17).

**Fact 27 differs by machine kind and both halves bind.** On a systemd rental, no elevated access is needed for the placement at all, and root is needed for everything about preparing the machine to render; passwordless root existed on the image under the cloud-init account rather than the login Smoozy runs as. On a container, nothing is open from a non-root login, and root inside is capability-bounded ([The last two boxes](The%20last%20two%20boxes%20—%20the%20hook%20that%20cannot%20be%20read%20back,%20the%20full%20disk,%20and%20the%20unit%20that%20kills%20a%20render.md) §10; Fact 27).

**Fact 49 is answered yes with one reading named wrong, and nothing below depends on that reading being right.** Cut at a recorded safe boundary, the machine named the third of five boundaries with the moment it was reached; cut hard, it named the second — the last boundary actually passed. The state survives a reboot with no worker behind it and holds admission back. **The wrong reading is the defunct-entry hole**: after a hard cut, with no setup worker alive, the machine reported the recorded pair as still validating until a reboot reaped it — the supervisor on that machine did not reap the workers it started (`Rented-server runtime` §16 item 4's rider, **closed 2026-08-22 by X9a's wait**). [S5](#4-interruption-resumption-and-the-safe-re-run) leans on that reading only in the direction that holds: it can report a dead group live, never a live one dead.

**The setup-in-progress state's mechanism is built and is not re-decided.** `Rented-server runtime` §9: this document sets and clears it through the ordinary command path and the supervisor never does (P3); setting it is durable before the restart a setup step causes (P4); **clearing it runs reconciliation** (P5), which is what re-runs the interrupted job after the setup rather than at the next supervisor start; no timer and no reconnection clears it (P7); it is reported on every health answer (V9).

**One login runs everything, and elevation is therefore never the worker's.** The supervisor and every worker it starts, the render included, run as one login (`Job files and outputs` L13). An elevated act is performed over the SSH connection by the machine's own elevating command, with the password on that command's standard input and never as an argument (`Server protocol and transfers` §1.3; `Security and licence` §4) — the route is fixed there and is not re-chosen here.

**Nothing here is keyed on a host key, on reachability, on a process name or pattern, or on a file's presence, name or size** (`Rented-server runtime` §15's closing rule; `State, identity and recovery` §1.3 rule 5). A recycled container answers on the same address with everything Smoozy installed destroyed; the installation identity of `State, identity and recovery` §2.3 is the only thing that tells a rental from an installation, and *"`Server Setup`'s re-run is what the user meets"* there. Nothing about what a machine holds is remembered across connections: readings here are period-held under D4, and **no desired state is ever inferred from an observed reading** (D2); readings taken on different clocks are never subtracted (D5).

**Do not read past the input documents.** Every machine observation quoted here is a frozen snapshot of **one to nine boxes on one to four days, across three sellers**: *"Read it as this is what that box did, never as this is what a rented machine does."* **Corrected 2026-08-23: this said *one to six boxes on one to three days*, the width before the multi-provider batch of 2026-08-22, while [Section 13](#13-unresolved) item 13 already carried the widened count — the widening reached the unresolved list and not this sentence. More boxes is still not a description of rented machines, which is the point of both.**

## 1. The machine kind, and what a setup can be on each

**Constraint:** Row 116, and Fact 27 on both weights. The detection is `Rented-server runtime` K1's and is not re-invented: `systemd-detect-virt` plus the container marker file, selecting by that closed table, re-taken at every placement and never carried forward (K2).

**M1. Every setup act below runs against the kind K1's table detected for the current installation.** A machine the table does not place is a machine no package is placed on (K3) and no setup runs on; the user is told the machine is not one Smoozy can set up.

**M1a. Beside the kind, every setup run reads the machine's *distribution family*, and a family outside the supported pair stops the run before anything is installed. Added 2026-08-22 on the Creator's ruling.** The spec now names **Ubuntu and Debian** as the families this module prepares ([Server Setup](../Specs/Server%20Setup.md), *Getting the machine ready*, Creator-stated 2026-08-22), and every step below that installs, repairs or removes anything is written in one family's terms — the driver route, the repair sequence, the frontend-lock setting, the package names. **So the family is a reading of the same standing as the kind, taken at the same moment, and a machine the pair does not hold is one no step of [R1](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of) is composed for.**

- **What is read, and from where.** `/etc/os-release`'s `ID`, with `ID_LIKE` read beside it and used only in the refusal's wording, and `/usr/lib/os-release` read only where the first is absent. **Documented** — `os-release(5)`, systemd 252.36, read 2026-08-22 at `https://manpages.debian.org/bookworm/systemd/os-release.5.en.html` (see [Sources](Sources/Server%20Setup.sources.md)). The file is the operating system's own identification — *"The /etc/os-release and /usr/lib/os-release files contain operating system identification data"* — and the precedence is the standard's own: *"The file /etc/os-release takes precedence over /usr/lib/os-release. Applications should check for the former, and exclusively use its data if it exists, and only fall back to /usr/lib/os-release if it is missing."* `ID` is *"A lower-case string … identifying the operating system, excluding any version information and suitable for processing by scripts"*, and `ID_LIKE` is *"A space-separated list of operating system identifiers … closely related to the local operating system in regards to packaging and programming interfaces"*, and the standard does say to consult it where `ID` is not recognised: *"Build scripts and similar should check this variable if they need to identify the local operating system and the value of ID= is not recognized."* **This document reads it and does not admit on it** — that is a narrowing of the standard's advice rather than a reading of it, taken because the promise names two families by name and this module's steps are written for those two. Its own example shows why the temptation was there — *"For an operating system with `ID=ubuntu`, an assignment of `ID_LIKE=debian` is appropriate."*
- **The test, and it is a fixed set rather than a pattern.** The run is composed where `ID` is `ubuntu` or `debian`, and nowhere else. **`ID_LIKE` is read only to say why a machine was refused**, never to admit one.
- **The Creator was asked on 2026-08-25 whether to widen it and ruled that the refusal stands.** A machine like Linux Mint or Pop!\_OS carries `ID_LIKE=ubuntu` and is Ubuntu underneath in every way this module touches, so admitting it would work — and it is refused all the same. **The grounds are the cost of being wrong against the chance of being asked**: the refusal installs nothing, changes nothing on the machine and costs one sentence, while admitting a family on `ID_LIKE` alone is a promise about machines nobody has run a setup on; and `The multi-provider batch` records 1,700 sampled offers on one provider as Ubuntu and nothing else. **Widening is a Specs change and stays the Creator's**, filed at `Preparation/Groundwork/Open Threads.md` under *Parked*.

- **A second arm was written on 2026-08-22 and taken out again the same day, at the stage-close gate, and the reason is worth keeping.** It admitted any machine whose `ID` was unrecognised and whose `ID_LIKE` named `debian` — meant to take an Ubuntu derivative without keeping a list of them. **Three things were wrong with it.** It was a pattern under a sentence calling itself a fixed set. It reached past what was ruled: the Creator ruled **Ubuntu and Debian**, and [Whole app](../Specs/Whole%20app.md)'s platform boundary says *"On a Linux machine of another family it says so plainly and prepares nothing"* while [Server Setup](../Specs/Server%20Setup.md)'s *Getting the machine ready* carries the same rule in its own words — *"where the answer is not one of the two, Smoozy says so plainly, prepares nothing, and leaves the machine exactly as it found it"* — and a machine admitted by that arm is by `ID` neither. **Corrected 2026-08-23: the verbatim sentence was attributed to both specs and belongs to one; the rule is in both, the words are `Whole app`'s.** **And it assigned such a machine no family at all** — so [M1b](#1-the-machine-kind-and-what-a-setup-can-be-on-each)'s per-family driver route, the repair sequence and the package names had nothing to select on, and the machine would have passed the gate only to meet the first step that differs between the two families with no rule for it. **That is a part-prepared machine, which is the exact outcome this gate exists to prevent.**

- **Whether derivatives should be admitted is a real question and it is not this stage's.** Admitting them widens what the user is promised, which is a Specs change and the Creator's; it is parked in `Preparation/Groundwork/Open Threads.md` rather than decided here. **Nothing is lost by refusing them today**: the refusal is one sentence, nothing is installed, and the machine is left exactly as found — and a user who meets it has rented a kind the spec told them about before they rented.
- **A reading that never reached the machine is not an answer, and a refusal reached through one is told as the connection going.** *Added 2026-10-26 by the builder of the worldwide pass's setup faults (`Audits/Build/Step 10c/Brief — the worldwide pass's setup faults.md`), a hole found on a real rental: an Ubuntu 22.04 container was told it was not a Linux server Smoozy supports at the moment a three-second reconnect took the connection from under these readings, and set up on the next press.* An absent answer is still never read as a family, or as systemd for M1 — but where a reading's command never reached the machine, or its file read found the channel gone, **the machine was not asked**, and saying it is a kind Smoozy does not prepare would be untrue (Rule 8). The run ends as [Y7](#33-the-worker-the-driving-and-the-stop)'s lost connection does — *"a connection lost part-way through stops it, and nothing resumes on its own"* — with nothing touched, since nothing is touched before these readings pass; `Servers`' own repair is what puts the connection back. Readings that did place the machine are not undone by one that did not. No auditor has seen it.
- **The file is parsed as the standard describes and never sourced as a shell script.** The standard permits sourcing and then says why that is not needed — *"beyond mere variable assignments, no shell features are supported … allowing applications to read the file without implementing a shell compatible execution engine"*. Sourcing a machine-written file would also be a machine-origin value entering a command, which `Server protocol and transfers` 1.1 forbids outright.
- **A machine that will not say is refused, never guessed at.** Where neither file can be read, or `ID` is absent, the family is **unknown** and the run is refused in the same words as an unsupported one. **An absent answer is never read as Ubuntu.** It is the reading discipline this stage already holds to everywhere: an empty answer is never inferred from a missing one.
- **What the user is told, and when.** One sentence at the moment the setup is asked for: this is a Linux kind Smoozy does not prepare, naming what it read where it read anything. **Nothing is installed, nothing is touched, and the machine is left exactly as found** — so the refusal is a reading rather than a part-finished run, and [Section 4](#4-interruption-resumption-and-the-safe-re-run)'s recovery machinery never engages, because no setup-in-progress state is ever set. The wording is Design's; that it is one sentence and not a failure report is the spec's.
- **Everything else about such a machine is untouched.** It connects, it is browsed, its Blenders are found by [B10](#54-the-search)'s search, and a job runs on it if it already carries what a render needs. **This module declines; the app does not.** That is the spec's own separation, and it is why the refusal sits here rather than at the first connection, which [Servers](../Specs/Servers.md) keeps for a machine that is not Linux at all.
- **How often the refusal will actually fire is a property of what sellers stock, and the first reading of that is on the record. Added 2026-08-22, observed.** [The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20%E2%80%94%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md) read three sellers' own image lists the same day. **One sells no Debian at all** — Ubuntu and AlmaLinux in every region — and delivered an Ubuntu machine. **One sells Ubuntu, Debian and AlmaLinux.** **One sells whatever image the renter names**, which makes a Debian container trivially available there and a Debian *virtual machine* not. **Nothing breaks and no rule changes**: the supported pair is what Smoozy prepares, not what any seller stocks, and every seller read stocks at least one of the two. **It is recorded because it is the first observation that a seller may not offer a family Smoozy supports** — so a user who wants the other one may not be able to rent it, and the refusal above is the ending they would meet if they rented a third family instead.
- **The family is re-read exactly as the kind is** — at every placement, never carried forward (K2). A recycled or re-imaged machine under a new installation identity has no remembered family.
- **And on a machine never prepared, both readings are taken before the hand-over rather than at it. Added 2026-08-22, out of the third sanity sweep's verification round.** [R2](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of)'s order puts [R7](#23-what-the-placement-is-given)'s elevated `chown` of the mount or mounts chosen, and the areas' creation, **before** the placement, so a family read at the placement would be read on a machine already changed, and *nothing is touched* would be false in the one case it is written for. **It costs nothing to move**: `/etc/os-release` is a plain read over the file-transfer subsystem, needing no elevation, nothing placed and no hand-over, and K1's kind detection is a command-channel reading of the same standing. **So the refusal happens before the first act that changes anything**, which is what makes it a reading rather than a part-finished run.

- **The pair names no version, and that binds what Smoozy places on the machine as well as what it reads. Added 2026-09-26, out of step 10c of Build; observed.** Nothing here admits a machine by its release of Ubuntu or Debian, so a machine this clause admits may be any release of either — and until 2026-09-23 **the program Smoozy placed on it ran on Ubuntu 24.04 and newer only**, having been built against the runner's own C library (release 2.39); a rented Ubuntu 22.04 machine, admitted here correctly, could not start it at all. **The pair stands as written and was not narrowed.** What moved is the artifact: `Server protocol and transfers` [B8c](Server%20protocol%20and%20transfers.md#5-the-bootstrap-and-repair-request-and-package-inspection) now requires it to carry its own C library and ask the machine for none, and the release tool refuses one that does. **What is still unestablished is the oldest kernel that program needs**, which no reading here takes; no machine on record has been old enough to meet it.

**M1b. Debian is not Ubuntu at the one step that matters, and each family has its own route. Added 2026-08-22; the gap it named was closed the same day by running it.** [R1](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of) item 5 installs a driver through **`ubuntu-drivers install --gpgpu`** on Ubuntu, which is Canonical's tool and exists nowhere else, and through **`nvidia-detect` then `apt-get install nvidia-driver`** on Debian, after `contrib non-free non-free-firmware` are enabled — Debian's own equivalent, run end to end on a rented Debian 12 machine ([The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20%E2%80%94%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md)). **So the driver step is written per family, and both families now have a route rather than one having a gap.** **Both routes begin by making their tool reachable — added 2026-09-21**: Debian by enabling its components, Ubuntu by installing `ubuntu-drivers-common`, which a plain Ubuntu image need not carry ([R1](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of) item 5 has the reading). **And every install on either route is preceded by a refresh of the package lists — added 2026-09-22** ([S7d](#43-why-each-step-is-safe-to-run-again)): a rented image's lists are as old as the image, and M1b's own Debian refresh follows the components step only because that step has just changed the sources. **This clause used to end *nothing establishes an equivalent on Debian, and this project has never run a driver install on a Debian machine*, with Debian's half deferred to [Section 13](#13-unresolved) item 22; that item is now closed.**

**Two things are per family beyond the command itself, and they are why *borrow Ubuntu's* would still have been wrong.** The Debian route needs **`contrib non-free non-free-firmware` enabled first**, because the stock image reaches `main` only and the driver is otherwise not merely uninstalled but unreachable. And the Debian route needs **a restart, unconditionally** — the headers for the kernel a stale image booted may not be in the archive at all, so the module is built against a newer kernel the machine is not running. **Everything else in the sequence is shared honestly rather than by assumption**: the package repair sequence is `dpkg`'s and apt's own and both quote Debian's own manuals already ([S7](#43-why-each-step-is-safe-to-run-again)); the frontend-lock setting is apt's ([S7b](#43-why-each-step-is-safe-to-run-again)), and the `unattended-upgrades` *default* behind it turns out to be a property of the **image** rather than of either family — **observed on both sides on 2026-08-22**: Hyperstack's `Debian 12 Plain` carries it installed and both timers enabled, and Vast's `ubuntu:24.04` container carries none of it, no configuration file and no timers. **So S7b's probe-rather-than-assume rule is vindicated in both directions**, and no family may be read as carrying or not carrying it; and `tar`'s Essential status is **Debian Policy's** rather than Ubuntu's, so item 3's reasoning holds on both. **The check-then-restart order is what keeps the unestablished half safe**: a driver that did not install fails `nvidia-smi` and the machine is reported not ready, which is [W4](#10-what-this-module-says-about-a-machine)'s refusal rather than a wrong render.

**M2. What each kind permits, taken from Fact 27 and Fact 48 rather than probed by trial:**

| | systemd machine (virtual or bare-metal) | container |
|---|---|---|
| package placement, boot registration | unprivileged, whole (K4 to K8) — **except** the boot-hook case does not arise | registration is the provider's hook and **installing it requires root** (K11); the root step must leave it readable per K19 |
| hand-over of a chosen mount that arrived `root:root` — the root's, and the package area's second mount where [R7](#23-what-the-placement-is-given) has one | elevated step, this document's ([Job files and outputs](Job%20files%20and%20outputs.md) 1.3; Fact 6) | same, where the login can elevate at all |
| unpack tool and shared libraries | elevated package installs | possible only where the login is the container's root (observed: a bare `ubuntu:22.04` container took them as root); refused outright to a non-root login (Fact 27) |
| driver install, repair, reload | elevated; may require a restart (Fact 80); **and replacement, where the driver answers below the OptiX bar of the newest Blender on the server, whenever it arrived — made only where nothing is rendering or waiting to render** ([R1](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of) item 5, [R1b](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of); added 2026-10-11, the Creator's rulings on OptiX and the driver; *widened later the same day from "of the newest Blender the run installs", the Creator's ruling that the driver follows every Blender on the server*) | **never** — all four routes closed, restart not a recovery action (Fact 48); **a driver below the bar is told to the user instead** (`Servers` H11b; added 2026-10-11) |
| machine restart | elevated (`systemctl reboot`; Fact 51) | never issued: it would restart the container, not the machine, and repairs nothing (Fact 48) |

**M3. Elevation is probed before it is offered, with the elevating command's non-interactive form.** Documented — `sudo(8)`, version 1.9.17p2, manual dated June 7, 2025, read 2026-08-19 on macOS 26.5.2 (see [Sources](Sources/Server%20Setup.sources.md)): `-n, --non-interactive` *"Avoid prompting the user for input of any kind. If a password is required for the command to run, sudo will display an error message and exit."* **And the release a rental actually ran is now read too, rather than assumed to say the same — added 2026-08-23.** `sudo(8)` at **1.9.15p5**, the version machine A carried, as Ubuntu publishes it for the image that shipped it — `https://manpages.ubuntu.com/manpages/noble/man8/sudo.8.html`, read 2026-08-23 — **gives this option word for word as above**. **This clause used to stand on the Creator's Mac's page at 1.9.17p2 alone, the rental's own release never having been read.** A probe that succeeds means passwordless elevation; one that fails means a password is owed and [E2](#9-elevation-the-refusal-and-the-password)'s flow begins at the moment a step needs it — never before (the spec's rule). **The probe has now been run on a rental in this form — 2026-08-22 — and it is no longer an assumption.** [The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20%E2%80%94%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md), machine A, under a purpose-made non-root login in group `sudo` with Ubuntu's stock sudoers: `sudo -n -v` returned **`sudo: a password is required`** at exit 1, and the same login's `sudo -S -v` with the right password returned **exit 0** having run no command. **This clause used to read *The probe was not run on any rental in this form and is labelled an assumption about the rentals' own `sudo`*.** The earlier rental observations stand beside it — `sudo -n` demanding a password on a container and passwordless sudo present on virtual-machine images (Fact 27). **One thing the probe could not be asked of the delivered login**: it arrives as root, `id -u` is 0 and `sudo -n true` returns 0, so no elevation question can be posed of it at all, which is why the run made a login to pose it with. [Section 13](#13-unresolved) item 6.

**REPAIRED 2026-09-04, at a build sitting on rented machines rather than at a round, and no auditor of this document has seen it. The probe's form changes: it is the non-interactive form of a command that does nothing — `sudo -n true` — and it is not the validate form `sudo -n -v`.** **Observed in a test** on two providers' standard images and two distribution families — Hyperstack `Debian 12 Plain` and Hyperstack `Ubuntu Server 22.04 LTS R570 CUDA 12.8`, each on the login its provider delivered, **2026-09-04** (see [Sources](Sources/Server%20Setup.sources.md)): **`sudo -n true` exited 0 on both**, while **`sudo -n -v` exited 1 with `sudo: a password is required` on both**. The cause was read on the machines rather than inferred: each login is granted **both** `(ALL : ALL) ALL`, which asks for a password, and `(ALL) NOPASSWD: ALL`, which does not, and validating meets the first. **So the validate form reported that a password was owed on machines that needed none**, and what followed was [E2](#9-elevation-the-refusal-and-the-password)'s password flow and a user asked for a password that does not exist — on the ordinary case, on two images out of two.

**Why the validate form was the wrong question rather than a wrong answer, and this half is documented.** `-v, --validate` is *"Update the user's cached credentials, authenticating the user if necessary… but does not run a command"* — it asks whether the login can **authenticate**, which is not what this clause needs to know. `-n, --non-interactive` on a command is *"If a password is required for the command to run, sudo will display an error message and exit"* — which **is** this clause's question, asked of a command. Both quotations are already this document's, at the readings above and in the companion.

**What this clause does not assert, stated because the evidence does not reach it.** A probe that succeeds says a password was not required **for the probe's own command**, and nothing further. A policy granting some commands without a password and refusing others would answer *passwordless* here and refuse a later step; the only sudoers policies this project has read are the two blanket ones above, on two machines on one day. **Nothing is built for that case and nothing needs to be:** [E4](#9-elevation-the-refusal-and-the-password) classifies a refused step from that step's own result, and [E2](#9-elevation-the-refusal-and-the-password)'s timing rule already puts the ask at the moment a step is refused rather than at the probe — so a probe that was over-optimistic costs a refusal, not a wrong statement to the user. **What would settle it:** `sudoers(5)` read as a publication on the per-command `NOPASSWD` tag, and one rental carrying a per-command policy. Neither has been done, and the Creator's ruling of 2026-08-31 leaves no rental for it. [Section 13](#13-unresolved) item 6.

**Ubuntu ships two elevating commands now, and this document rested on one implementation's manual until 2026-08-21. The three quotations below used to carry a read date of 2026-08-20 and no address at all, evidenced nowhere but here; the publications were read on 2026-08-23 and the addresses are recorded** (see [Sources](Sources/Server%20Setup.sources.md)). Ubuntu 25.10 and 26.04 install **sudo-rs** by default — **that project's own README states it** and nothing else read here does: `https://raw.githubusercontent.com/trifectatechfoundation/sudo-rs/main/README.md`, read 2026-08-23, under its *Ubuntu 25.10 (Questing Quokka) and 26.04 (Resolute Raccoon)* heading — *"sudo-rs is installed and enabled by default; you can control which sudo version is being used by running `update-alternatives --config sudo`"*. **Corrected 2026-08-23: this clause used to say Canonical's own page confirms the default install, and it does not** — that page states only *"This is a list of major differences as of the Ubuntu 25.10 and 26.04 releases, to help users upgrading to those."* **Nothing rests on the second attribution**; the default-install claim stands on the project's own README. **Both forms this document uses are in sudo-rs's own manual too** — `https://raw.githubusercontent.com/trifectatechfoundation/sudo-rs/main/docs/man/sudo.8.md`, read 2026-08-23: `-n, --non-interactive` — *"Avoid prompting the user for input of any kind. If any input is required for the command to run, sudo-rs will display an error message and exit."* — and `-v, --validate` — *"Update the session record for the current session, authenticating the user if necessary."* **So neither form is at risk.** **What does differ is the password prompt's own text**, and that half of Canonical's page holds word for word — Ubuntu Server documentation, *sudo-rs*, `https://ubuntu.com/server/docs/reference/other-tools/sudo-rs/`, read 2026-08-23: *"sudo.ws prompt for password says `[sudo] password for <USERNAME>`, whereas sudo-rs prompt says `[sudo: authenticate] <METHOD>:`."* **Nothing here breaks on that**, because [E2](#9-elevation-the-refusal-and-the-password) hands the password on standard input and matches no prompt — but it is recorded because anything at Build that matched on the prompt text would break on Ubuntu 25.10 and later. **sudo-rs has never been run on a rental**, so item 6's *never run* half is untouched by this. *(Narrowed 2026-09-02 at the stage-close gate repair: this read* Neither implementation has been run on a rental*, which **M3** just above and [Section 13](#13-unresolved) item 6 both contradict — the probe has been run on the classic implementation, and it is only the second that has not.)*

**M4. The login Smoozy holds is the connection's login and no other.** Smoozy knows servers and SSH keys, never providers, so an account it has no credential for — the cloud-init account holding passwordless root on one observed image — is not a route Smoozy has. Where the connection's login has no elevation route, the machine's preparation cannot be performed, which is Row 116's own clause: *"a machine that refuses it may not be set up at all."* That is a narrowing the spec already carries, not a hole.

---

## 2. The ready action

**Constraint:** Row 88, quoted in the row file with its `[PENDING]` marker naming this stage; Facts 47, 80, 27 and 48, all answered. The action promises the outcome — the machine made able to render — reached by a single action; what that action is differs by machine kind, and pretending otherwise would be wrong on the container kind, which is observed rather than assumed (Fact 48). **Narrowed 2026-08-23; this used to read *wrong on the commonest rental kind*, which is one seller's claim about its own instances stated as a property of rentals.** Fact 48's *"Instances are almost always Docker containers; a small subset are virtual machines"* is **Vast.ai's own documentation about Vast.ai**, and the three-seller batch of 2026-08-22 delivered **virtual machines on the other two sellers** ([The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20%E2%80%94%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md)). **Nothing here turns on which kind is commoner**: every rule in this document is written per kind ([M2](#1-the-machine-kind-and-what-a-setup-can-be-on-each)) off a reading taken at every placement and never carried forward (K2). **The constraint stands: the single action is composed per kind either way.**

### 2.1 What the action is made of

**R1. The ready action is one action to the user and a fixed, ordered sequence of steps underneath, composed per machine at run start from what the machine is found to need.** It runs as a setup run under [Section 3](#3-the-setup-run-its-worker-and-the-recorded-safe-boundaries)'s discipline: the machine-level setup-in-progress state is set before its first machine-changing step, each completed step is a recorded safe boundary, and the state is cleared only at the end, which runs reconciliation. The steps, in order, each present only where the machine needs it:

1. **Readings.** The machine kind (M1); card presence read from the PCI vendor scan — `lspci -d 10de:`, empty at exit 0 for an absent vendor — and **never** from the card tool's absence, which is byte-identical to a cardless machine's answer (Fact 46). On a container, card presence is read from the driver's own answer alone, because `/proc/driver/nvidia` and the PCI scan are inherited from the host and lie (`Rented-server runtime` V6). **And the scan may not be runnable at all — added 2026-08-22, observed.** `lspci` was **absent from every container image in [The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20%E2%80%94%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md)** — three boxes on one seller — and present on the virtual machine; `pciutils` is not in those images and installing it is a package operation this reading is taken before. **So *the scan returned empty* and *the scan did not run* are two different readings and are never collapsed**, because only the first concludes *no card*. `Servers` H11's fifth row is the rule; where the scan did not run **and the driver does not answer either**, this step reports the cards as not established rather than absent. **Where the driver answers, the cards are established and the scan's absence costs nothing** — H11's first row, narrowed 2026-08-23, because the driver's own query was honest on all three of those boxes. **A machine that is not a container and lacks `lspci` too is uncovered by the container branch above and is `Servers` H12b's case.**
2. **Fetch**, where the run installs a Blender ([B4](#51-the-list-and-the-fetch)): the archive staged on the machine, resumable, digest-checked. Unprivileged.
3. **Tooling.** The one unpack tool Blender's documentation names, installed where absent (elevated), and the archive unpacked into staging with it and nothing else (Row 115). The tool was present on the one box read for it; a machine lacking it is the case this step exists for. **ANSWERED 2026-08-22 by observation, and the park closes.** This used to read *How likely that case is turns on which tool it is, and that half is a Blender question this project cannot answer with its own documents open — named 2026-08-21 and parked.* **It needed no documentation.** [The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20%E2%80%94%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md): Blender ships as **`.tar.xz`** — the two archives fetched were 377 902 364 and 384 441 228 bytes — and **`xz` was absent from the image**, so the archive could not be unpacked until `xz-utils` was installed. **So the step is realistic rather than close to unreachable, and it is the `xz` case that is the common one.** What is settled is the packaging on either side. **Every page behind it was read on 2026-08-23 and every clause of it held; it used to be stated as settled on pages no record of which existed anywhere in the project** (see [Sources](Sources/Server%20Setup.sources.md)). `tar` **cannot** be absent from an ordinary Ubuntu, because it is **Essential** — Ubuntu's own package page tags the package `[essential]` (`https://packages.ubuntu.com/noble/tar`, read 2026-08-23; Debian's tags it identically at `https://packages.debian.org/bookworm/tar`) — and Debian Policy §3.8 defines Essential as *"the minimal set of functionality that must be available and usable on the system at all times, even when packages are in the 'Unpacked' state"*, removable only under a force option: *"Since these packages cannot be easily removed (one has to specify an extra force option to `dpkg` to do so), this flag must not be used unless absolutely necessary."* (Debian Policy Manual v4.7.4.1, `https://www.debian.org/doc/debian-policy/ch-binary.html#essential-packages`, read 2026-08-23). `xz-utils` **can** be absent: its Ubuntu page carries no such tag (`https://packages.ubuntu.com/noble/xz-utils`, read 2026-08-23), and `tar`'s own page lists it under *suggests* rather than *depends*. **They need `xz`, so this step is realistic, and that is what sizes it, [Y1](#4-interruption-resumption-and-the-safe-re-run) and [W3](#10-what-this-module-says-about-a-machine)'s warning-table row honestly** — **corrected 2026-08-22: this closed on the pre-answer conditional *if Blender's archives need only `tar`, this step is close to unreachable; if they need `xz`, it is realistic*, whose antecedent the observation two sentences above settles.** **Nothing is blocked either way**: the step probes rather than assumes, and installing a tool that was already there costs nothing.
4. **Libraries.** `ldd` is run over the Blender executable the machine will render with — the staged build where this run fetched one, otherwise each build [B10](#54-the-search)'s search found — and **the whole not-found list is read at once**; the named packages are installed explicitly (elevated); `ldd` is re-read and must come back clean. Never one library at a time, never from the launch error, never left to the driver's dependencies (Fact 47). **Where this run fetches no Blender and the search finds none, the step has no subject and does not run**: there is nothing on the machine to read a library chain against, and the action declines the library half and says so ([W1](#10-what-this-module-says-about-a-machine)) rather than composing a fetch nobody asked for. The half is re-reached the moment a Blender arrives, because a run that installs one runs this step over the build it stages.

   **The first real entry for the fixed build-time library-to-package mapping [E7](#9-elevation-the-refusal-and-the-password) requires. Added 2026-08-22, observed.** On a stock `ubuntu:24.04` container the Blender binary failed with `error while loading shared libraries: libX11.so.6: cannot open shared object file: No such file or directory`, and the packages that resolved the whole chain were **ten**: `libx11-6 libxi6 libxxf86vm1 libxfixes3 libxrender1 libgl1 libegl1 libsm6 libice6 libxkbcommon0`. **Installing all ten took 17 seconds.** [The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20%E2%80%94%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md). **This is one image on one seller and it is the mapping's first entry rather than the mapping**: the list is a property of the image and E7's requirement is that it be fixed at build time rather than derived from a launch error, which is what this step already does.

   **AN ENTRY IS KEYED ON EVERY LIBRARY ITS OWN PACKAGES PUBLISH, AND UNTIL 2026-09-22 THIS ONE WAS KEYED ON THE LAUNCH ERROR'S NAME ALONE. Added 2026-09-22 by the build runner, out of step 10c's fourth pass on Vast, where it stopped every Blender install.** The entry above records the name Blender's launch error carried, and Blender names only the first library it cannot find — which is the very reason this step reads `ldd` instead. **A bare `ubuntu:24.04` container reports nine distinct libraries, not one**: `libX11.so.6`, `libXrender.so.1`, `libXfixes.so.3`, `libXi.so.6`, `libxkbcommon.so.0`, `libSM.so.6`, `libICE.so.6`, `libXext.so.6`, `libGL.so.1`, read over a fetched 5.2.2 on 2026-09-22. **Eight of the nine had no key, so the step refused and installed nothing — while the ten packages beside the key were the packages those eight needed.** Every test of it passed `libX11.so.6` alone, so the desk could not see it and four passes on rented machines were needed to. **So the rule this clause states is that an entry answers for every library its packages publish**, and which library a package publishes is read from the distribution's own file index rather than guessed from a name: eleven searches of `https://packages.ubuntu.com/search?searchon=contents&mode=exactfilename&suite=noble&arch=amd64`, read 2026-09-22, each answering with one package under `/usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/` — `libx11-6`, `libxi6`, `libxxf86vm1`, `libxfixes3`, `libxrender1`, `libgl1`, `libegl1`, `libsm6`, `libice6`, `libxkbcommon0` for the ten already named, and **`libxext6` for `libXext.so.6`, which no package in the list of 2026-08-22 publishes and which this clause now names explicitly** rather than leaving to another package's dependencies (`https://packages.ubuntu.com/noble/amd64/libxext6/filelist`, read 2026-09-22, which lists `/usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/libXext.so.6`). **Nothing else about the entry moves**: it is still one image's reading, the whole not-found list is still read at once, and [E7](#9-elevation-the-refusal-and-the-password)'s *selects, and never composes* is still what makes the install one string fixed at build time — an entry that answers for every one of a machine's not-found names selects exactly one command, and a list that reached across two entries would still fail honestly.

   **A library Blender opens by name while it runs is looked up by the machine's loader, not by `ldd`, and a library the loader does not find joins the not-found list at both reads; `libEGL.so.1` is the first such library. Added 2026-10-11 by the build runner, out of the Creator's eighth notes, note 5.** `ldd` lists only what the executable links, and over 5.2.2 on a bare `ubuntu:24.04` container it never named `libEGL.so.1` (the reading of 2026-09-22 above; **observed**), while a render's error stream on a RunPod machine said *Couldn't open libEGL.so.1* (note 5; **observed**) — so an image carrying every linked library but not this one read clean and kept the gap. The lookup is the one `Rendering` L24b makes for the OptiX library, shared rather than copied; the library is keyed in the entry above, whose `libegl1` publishes it on 22.04 as on 24.04 (Ubuntu's file lists for `jammy`, read 2026-10-11; citation in the sources companion); a lookup that gives no answer adds nothing. **It claims only that the library is present**: whether its absence stopped any render is not established.

   **What the fetch and the unpack cost, timed on the same box.** Download **5 s** for 4.5.12 and **4 s** for 5.2.0; unpack **62 s** and **67 s** on four cores, single-threaded `xz`. **The unpack dominates the download by an order of magnitude.** On a machine with more cores both archives downloaded in parallel and both unpacked in **34 seconds in all**. **So the cost of this step is the machine's processor rather than its link**, which is the opposite of what a transfer-shaped estimate would predict, and anything that shows the user a duration for it must not budget it as a transfer.
5. **Driver**, systemd kind only, and in this order: **check first** — `nvidia-smi` run and its exit read; where it answers, nothing is installed and nothing is touched. Where it fails and a card is present on the bus: where a driver is already installed, **the restart is tried before any install** — the two observed failure mechanisms (a card still bound to `nouveau`; a module built for a kernel not yet booted) are both fixed by the boot, and a reinstall on a working-but-unbooted install silently becomes an upgrade the running kernel does not match ([The rented machine](The%20rented%20machine.md)). **A driver counts as installed for this rule only where the running kernel has its module. Added 2026-09-30, found at Build by step 10c's eleventh pass.** On Hyperstack a driver's utilities stood on the machine with no kernel module for any kernel, `nvidia-smi` answered *driver not loaded*, and every re-run took the restart before any install, restarted, failed the check, and ended — so no press in Smoozy could mend the machine. The restart fixes an unbooted module; it cannot fix an absent one. **So where the check fails and the running kernel has no module for the driver, the install route runs**, the same route a bare machine takes. How the module's presence is read is the builder's, from the tool's own publication, cited at the site. Where no driver is installed, **the driver is installed by the distribution's own detection route rather than by a flavour this document names**, and **that route is per family ([M1b](#1-the-machine-kind-and-what-a-setup-can-be-on-each))**. **On Ubuntu** it is `ubuntu-drivers install --gpgpu` (elevated), Ubuntu Server's own documented and recommended command, *"which will install the driver that is considered the best match for your hardware"* (`https://ubuntu.com/server/docs/how-to/graphics/install-nvidia-drivers/`, read 2026-08-20) — then the check is run again. **Corrected 2026-09-21, found at Build: the Ubuntu route begins, like the Debian one, by making its tool reachable.** `ubuntu-drivers` is shipped in the package `ubuntu-drivers-common` — Ubuntu's own package index lists `/usr/bin/ubuntu-drivers` among that package's files for both 24.04 and 22.04 (read 2026-09-21; label: **documented**, citation in the sources companion) — and **a plain Ubuntu image need not carry it**: the second pass of step 10c met Hyperstack's plain 24.04 image without it, *"sudo: ubuntu-drivers: command not found"*, with the package available and not installed (label: **observed**, one image). **So on Ubuntu the route is two elevated acts in order: `apt-get -o DPkg::Lock::Timeout=120 install -y ubuntu-drivers-common`, then `ubuntu-drivers install --gpgpu`** — the first under S7b's bounded wait like every other package step, and a no-op on an image that already carries the tool. **This rule had only the Debian half of *make the route reachable first***, and nobody had checked the same thing for Ubuntu because every Ubuntu image this project had read carried the tool. Where the first act is refused the step ends there in the machine's own words, and neither the install nor the restart goes out — the same rule the Debian components step keeps. **Corrected 2026-09-22, found at Build by the third pass: [S7d](#43-why-each-step-is-safe-to-run-again)'s refresh of the package lists goes in front of the first act.** On Hyperstack's plain 24.04 image the tool's package was named by lists a year old and the archive no longer held that version, so the install answered *"404  Not Found"*. **So on Ubuntu the route is three elevated acts in order — the lists refreshed, `ubuntu-drivers-common` installed, `ubuntu-drivers install --gpgpu` — and a refresh that does not end cleanly ends the step the same way a refused tool install does.** The correction of 2026-09-21 had rejected the refresh because the one image read then had a candidate without it; the next image did not. **CORRECTED 2026-09-23, found at Build by the fifth pass, and this is the one that made the route finish: the Ubuntu arm is four elevated acts, and the fourth is the driver support binaries this clause's own check is run from.** On Hyperstack's plain 24.04 image the three acts above ran to their end for the first time anywhere — the lists refreshed, the tool installed at `1:0.9.7.6ubuntu3.7`, thirteen packages placed by `ubuntu-drivers install --gpgpu`, the kernel module loaded, `/dev/nvidia0` there and `/proc/driver/nvidia/gpus/…/information` naming the card — **and `nvidia-smi` was on the machine nowhere**, `apt-cache policy nvidia-utils-595-server` reading *Installed: (none)*. **So the whole of this item's check, and `Servers` H11's driver column with it, had nothing to run: a machine whose driver Smoozy had just installed went on reporting that Smoozy could not read its graphics cards, and *card answering* was unreachable on every Ubuntu machine Smoozy installs a driver on.** **The publication had said so all along and this route had stopped one command short of it.** Ubuntu Server's own page, under the very heading this clause quotes, prints `sudo ubuntu-drivers install --gpgpu` and then *"You will also want to install the following additional components:"* — `sudo apt install nvidia-utils-535-server` (`https://documentation.ubuntu.com/server/how-to/graphics/install-nvidia-drivers/`, read 2026-09-23). **And the binary is in no other package**: Ubuntu's own file index, searched for the exact filename in `noble` on `amd64`, answers *"Found 1 results"* for `/usr/bin/nvidia-smi` and names only the branch-numbered `nvidia-utils-…` packages, with no unversioned one, no virtual one and no `-open` variant (read 2026-09-23; both citations in the [Sources](Sources/Server%20Setup.sources.md) companion). **The branch is the detection route's own choice and is never named here**, which is this clause's standing rule and the reason a fixed list of branch numbers is refused: twenty-two of them are in `noble-updates` today and the next one ships without asking. **So the fourth act takes the branch the install has just put on the machine — read on the machine, from the driver package the install left, inside the command, under [E7](#9-elevation-the-refusal-and-the-password)'s fifth carriage — and installs that branch's utilities.** *Corrected 2026-09-30, found at Build by step 10c's eleventh pass: this read "asks the machine's own copy of the same tool which driver it recommends — `ubuntu-drivers list --gpgpu --recommended`, whose one printed line names the driver package the install itself would choose". On a machine that already carried another branch the install chose 580 and the list said 595, and the utilities of one removed the module of the other.* Which package the install left, and how it is read, is the builder's, from Ubuntu's and Debian's own publications, cited at the site. Nothing of it crosses from Smoozy. **Where no branch is read the act asks for no package and apt says so in its own words, and the step ends there** — no partial install and no restart, since a restart cannot put a missing package on a machine, which is the same rule the tool install keeps. **The act goes out whatever the install answered**, because a machine whose driver was already on it gets *"All the available drivers are already installed."* at exit 0 and is the other way a machine reaches this clause with a working driver and no check to run against it. **Debian is untouched**: that family's route installs the `nvidia-driver` metapackage its own detection tool names, and the run of 2026-08-22 read the driver, the card and its size back off `nvidia-smi` after the restart, so on that family the install already puts the check on the machine. **And this was not a new fact.** [Facts](Facts.md) Fact 47 has recorded since 2026-08-16 that this exact command on a Hyperstack image *"does not install `nvidia-smi`"*, and the [Sources](Sources/Server%20Setup.sources.md) companion has carried it under this item's own entry since 2026-08-23 as something the page does not mention — **it was written down twice, under this clause, and built against neither time.** **ADDED 2026-10-03, with the Creator's ruling that NVIDIA cards render with OptiX (`Rendering` L23): the Ubuntu arm is five elevated acts, and the fifth puts the OptiX library on the machine.** The same command on a Hyperstack image left `libnvoptix` out as well as `nvidia-smi` — [Facts](Facts.md) Fact 47, observed, one image — and with `OPTIX` named, every cards job on such a machine would end before the render instruction (`Rendering` L24b). **So after the fourth act the library is looked for by the same lookup `Rendering` L24b makes, and where it is not found, the package of the same branch that carries `libnvoptix.so.1` is installed**, the branch read on the machine exactly as the fourth act reads it, under [E7](#9-elevation-the-refusal-and-the-password)'s fifth carriage. **Which package carries the file is the builder's, from Ubuntu's own file index searched for that exact file name, cited at the site** — the same search that found the fourth act's package — and never a fixed list of branch numbers. Where the install is refused the step ends in apt's own words, like every other act here. **Where the library is still not found after it, the step does not end the setup**: the driver answers and a processor job renders, and `Rendering` L24b says it in plain words when a cards job reaches the machine. **Debian is not touched by this act** — whether its `nvidia-driver` metapackage carries the library is not established ([Section 13](#13-unresolved) item 27), and L24b is what a Debian machine without it meets. **And a driver that already answered is still never touched** (*check first*, above): a machine whose own image carried a driver without the library is L24b's case, not this act's. *Since 2026-11-02 (the Creator's ruling that setup checks for it) such a machine is also told from the first connection, by `Servers` H11b's OptiX-library bullet, and still nothing is installed on it; a library this act could not put on is told the same way at the next reading. No auditor has seen it.* **Naming a flavour here is forbidden, and this replaces a fixed rule that named the proprietary one.** NVIDIA's own README for driver 580.105.08, `https://download.nvidia.com/XFree86/Linux-x86_64/580.105.08/README/kernel_open.html`, read 2026-08-20: *"The proprietary flavor supports the GPU architectures Maxwell, Pascal, Volta, Turing, and later GPUs until Blackwell. Blackwell and later are only supported by the open kernel modules."* and *"The open kernel modules cannot support GPUs before Turing, because the open kernel modules depend on the GPU System Processor (GSP) first introduced in Turing."* **Neither flavour serves every card**, so any fixed rule fails on one end of the range. The observation behind the old rule stands and is the pre-Turing case NVIDIA documents — [The last two boxes](The%20last%20two%20boxes%20—%20the%20hook%20that%20cannot%20be%20read%20back,%20the%20full%20disk,%20and%20the%20unit%20that%20kills%20a%20render.md) §11 records the kernel's own words, *"installed in this system is not supported by open nvidia.ko because it does not include the required GPU System Processor (GSP)"* — and [The rented machine](The%20rented%20machine.md) §9 records the opposite end, a box driven by `nvidia-driver-580-open`. **On Debian the equivalent exists and was run end to end on 2026-08-22** ([The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20%E2%80%94%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md)), so this step is composed on that family too. **This replaces the previous rule, which read: *On Debian there is no established equivalent of that command and this project has run no driver install there*, and composed the step with no driver install on that family.** The route, in order, on a `Debian 12` image delivered with no driver at all — `nvidia-smi` absent, zero NVIDIA modules loaded, `dkms` absent:

   1. **The image enables only `main`**, so `apt-cache policy nvidia-driver` returns `Candidate: (none)` — the driver is unreachable rather than merely uninstalled. **`contrib non-free non-free-firmware` are enabled and the lists updated** before anything else. (`ubuntu-drivers-common` is confirmed absent and unpackaged on Debian: `apt-cache policy` returned nothing at all.)
   2. **`nvidia-detect` is Debian's own equivalent of Canonical's tool**, in `non-free`. Run against the card it printed `Detected NVIDIA GPUs:` … `Your card is supported by the default drivers.` … `It is recommended to install the` / `    nvidia-driver` / `package.` — **it names the package to install, which is exactly what the Ubuntu tool is used for.**
   3. **`apt-get install nvidia-driver`** (elevated), the package `nvidia-detect` named.
   4. **The restart is not optional on this family**, and step 6 below is where it happens. **The trap that makes it mandatory is in no manual:** the running kernel was `6.1.0-31-amd64` and **`linux-headers-6.1.0-31-amd64` is not in the archive at all** — Debian removes superseded kernel headers, so the headers for the kernel a stale image booted may simply not exist. The install resolved it by pulling a newer kernel and **DKMS built against `6.1.0-52-amd64`**, not against the running one. Until the boot, `nvidia-smi` reports `NVIDIA-SMI has failed because it couldn't communicate with the NVIDIA driver.` and `modprobe nvidia` reports `FATAL: Module nvidia-current not found in directory /lib/modules/6.1.0-31-amd64` — **the route looks broken and is not.**
   5. **After the restart the check passes**: kernel `6.1.0-52-amd64`, driver `535.261.03`, the card named and sized, and `nvidia_uvm`, `nvidia_drm`, `nvidia_modeset` and `nvidia` all loaded. **149 seconds for the install plus one restart — about four minutes from a bare card to a working card query, with nothing asked of a human.**

   **What this costs the architecture is the restart**, which makes [Section 8](#8-the-restart) and the whole-app boundary's power carve-out load-bearing on this family rather than incidental. **What it does not do is remove the check-then-restart order**: a driver that did not install still fails `nvidia-smi` and the machine is still reported not ready. **What the detection route does on a Blackwell card is not established** and is [Section 13](#13-unresolved) item 18; the check-then-restart order below is what catches it either way, because a wrong flavour fails the check rather than rendering wrongly. **Never a module unload and reload in place of the restart** — the observed result oscillates and poisons every later reading (Fact 80).
   **ADDED 2026-10-11, the Creator's rulings on OptiX and the driver (`Audits/Build/Step 10c/The Creator's rulings on OptiX and the driver, 2026-10-11.md`): a driver that answers is also read for its version, and where Smoozy can manage the driver it is brought up to the OptiX bar of the newest Blender on the server, with no warning.** *Widened 2026-10-11, later the same day, the Creator's ruling that the driver follows every Blender on the server (the same file, its last section): this read "of the newest Blender the run installs". That narrowing was the runner's own when recording the rulings, not the Creator's words. Where the swap is made, and when it waits, is [R1b](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of).*
   - **The reading.** Where `nvidia-smi` answers, the driver's version and the CUDA version it reports are read with it — the same reading `Servers` H11b takes. The query `nvidia-smi --query-gpu=driver_version --format=csv,noheader` gave the version on every machine of the OptiX check (**observed**, `Audits/Build/Step 10c/Making OptiX work — what we found.md`).
   - **The bar** is `Rendering` L23a's, for the newest Blender on the server: every build [B10](#54-the-search)'s search finds, and the build this run stages where its version is known before the step, from the list ([B4](#51-the-list-and-the-fetch)). L23a's table rises with the version, so that is the highest bar any of them has. **A run that installs no Blender compares against the Blenders already there.** Where no Blender is on the server and none is staged, nothing is compared and *check first* stands as it did. A build whose version is known only when it answers ([B7](#52-a-build-the-user-points-at)) is compared after publication, by [R1b](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of). *Changed 2026-10-11, later the same day, the Creator's ruling that the driver follows every Blender on the server: this read "for the newest Blender this run installs — the version is known before the step, from the list (B4) or the build's own answer (B7). **A run that installs no Blender compares nothing**, and *check first* stands as it did."*
   - **Where anything on the server is rendering or waiting to render, the swap is not made in this run, and it waits** ([R1b](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of)). The rest of the run is unchanged. *Added 2026-10-11, the same ruling.* *A job held for the swap ([R1c](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of)) is not counted as waiting here. Added 2026-10-11, later still, the Creator's ruling that a job needing the newer driver is held back.*
   - **Below the bar, on a systemd machine, the step does work: a newer driver goes on**, by the family's own route, and the check after it passes only where the driver answers **at or above the bar**. Otherwise [step 6](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of)'s restart follows, and the check runs again on return — the same check-then-restart rule as every other driver act here. **Nothing asks the user and nothing warns them about the driver.** The step list says *done*, as for any install. **The swap never adds to [W3](#10-what-this-module-says-about-a-machine)'s warning**, because it is made only where nothing is live ([R1b](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of)); a run with other driver work in it still names the jobs a restart would take down. *Changed 2026-10-11, later the same day, the Creator's ruling that the driver follows every Blender on the server: this read "W3's warning about live jobs is a different thing and is unchanged: a run with driver work in it still names the jobs a restart would take down."*
   - **Two things the check met on a provider's ready-made image are this step's to handle.** On Hyperstack's R550 image, replacing the image's own driver waited about ten minutes on the image's own package upgrades, and met two package conflicts between the image's driver packages and the new branch (**observed**, one image; `Making OptiX work — what we found.md`, *What Smoozy should do*). **How the old driver gives way is the builder's**, from the distribution's own publications, cited at the site. The bounded wait of [S7b](#43-why-each-step-is-safe-to-run-again) already covers a package system held by its own upgrades. Smoozy's own route on plain Ubuntu 24.04 chose 595.91.07 (**observed**), which is above every bar in the table. *The swap's own 580.178.04 meets the table's top row, 580 from 5.3.0 (`Rendering` L23a), and does not pass it. Added 2026-11-02.*
   - **Where the swap does not reach the bar, nothing is forced and setup does not fail for it.** Where the route is refused, the install fails, or the check reads a driver that answers but is still below the bar, the run carries on, and *ready* claims what [R4](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of) claims, which never included OptiX. The user is told in `Servers` H11b's words, and the step record carries that the bar was not reached. **Where the driver no longer answers at all**, that is this item's existing failure, the card never answering, and the ending is *Could not be made ready*.
   - **How the newer driver goes on, on Ubuntu. Added 2026-10-11 at Build, the OptiX and the driver work's third piece.** Not by the detection route, because the swap removes a working driver and must know before it does that the new one can go on, at a branch above the bar, with its fabric manager: the detection route names its branch only as it installs. And the package conflicts on Hyperstack's R550 image came from packages out of NVIDIA's own archive, which that image carries and which publishes no `-server` package names (**observed**; `Audits/Build/Step 10c/OptiX and the driver, piece O3 — what landed.md`). **So the swap installs Ubuntu's own 580 server driver, `nvidia-driver-580-server` or `nvidia-driver-580-server-open`, in the flavour the machine already runs** — 580.178.04 on 22.04 and 24.04 (label: documented, Ubuntu's package index), the flavour read from the loaded module (label: documented, NVIDIA's own README). That is not a fixed flavour, which this item forbids: a card whose driver answers already runs on that flavour. Before anything is removed, a trial run of the whole install must pass; where it does not, nothing is touched. Debian has no swap (§13 item 28).
   - **An NVSwitch machine is never left without its fabric manager. Added 2026-10-11, the build runner's ruling.** Where the machine's fabric manager package is installed, the swap puts on the new branch's own fabric manager from Ubuntu's own archive with the driver, and where Ubuntu's archive has none for that release, the swap does not happen and is the swap that missed the bar, told to the user as above.
   - **The removal on an image that carries NVIDIA's own package archive. Added 2026-11-03 at Build, out of the Sesterce live pass (`Audits/Build/Blender 5.3/The live pass — Sesterce — what happened.md`, new fault 1); no auditor has seen this text.** On `ubuntu22.04_cuda12.8_shade_os` the removal of 570 also installed `libnvidia-cfg1` and upgraded `nvidia-persistenced`, both 615.71.10 from that archive, and stopped at dpkg's refusal of `/usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/libnvidia-cfg.so.1`, which `libnvidia-cfg1-570` also claimed, with four packages gone and no `nvidia-smi` (**observed**). The same refusal is on the record from Hyperstack's R550 image (`OptiX and the driver, piece O3 — what landed.md`), where it was answered on the install alone. **So the removal carries `-o Dpkg::Options::=--force-overwrite` as the install does**, and the same removal with that option ended at exit 0 on the half-removed machine (**observed**, by hand). **The trial run now covers the removal as well, as far as a trial can**: the same `apt … --purge remove` with `-s`, after the install's trial and before anything is touched, so a removal apt's resolver refuses leaves the old driver standing. **It cannot see this fault and nothing here pretends it can**: a simulation is `apt-get(8)`'s *"No action"*, printing *"a series of lines, each representing a dpkg operation"*, so dpkg never meets the file (documented; citation in the sources companion). The option is the answer to the file, and the trial is the answer to the resolver. **Assumption, labelled**: `apt` takes `-s` as `apt-get` does; where it does not, the trial fails, nothing is removed and the swap has missed the bar. **What stands afterwards, from the same pass's own listing after the hand repair and nothing else**: `libnvidia-cfg1` 615.71.10 installed beside `libnvidia-cfg1-580-server`, `nvidia-persistenced` 615.71.10 removed with its settings left (`rc`), and the driver answering 580.178.04 and rendering with OptiX (**observed**, one machine, with the install run on the half-removed machine rather than a fresh one). Which package's copy of that one file stands is **not read**. **What breaks if it matters**: a later upgrade of `libnvidia-cfg1` from that archive could replace it under the 580 driver. **What holds**: the check and the first cards job read the driver as they read any other.
   - **A driver left half-removed is finished, and the step never reads *done* over nothing. Added 2026-11-03 at Build, the same pass; no auditor has seen this text.** A *Set up again* on that machine took the install route, printed *0 upgraded, 0 newly installed*, read *done* and restarted for no gain (**observed**). **So where the check fails on Ubuntu and no restart is owed, the step first reads what the package system holds, unelevated and in one command**: whether a driver package stands (`nvidia-driver-<branch>…` or `nvidia-headless-no-dkms-<branch>…`, installed), and, where none does, the driver's kernel half left behind (`nvidia-dkms-<branch>…` or `nvidia-kernel-source-<branch>…`, in any state but removed). **Kernel half with no driver package is a removal that stopped part-way, and the step finishes it**: one elevated act, the swap's own trial, removal and install with the branch read from those package names and the flavour from their `-open`, so it needs no `nvidia-smi` and no loaded module. Item 6's restart and the check follow as after the swap. **Where that act fails the step fails in its own words, and no restart goes out.** **Every other reading goes the way it went**: a driver package standing, nothing left behind, or a reading that could not be had takes the detection route. **After the detection route, where the check still fails and the same reading finds no driver package at all, the step fails, says so, and no restart goes out**, because a restart cannot put a missing package on a machine. Where a driver package stands the restart still follows, because the module may be built for a kernel not yet booted. **Not established, with what breaks**: a removal that stopped after the kernel half went reads as nothing left behind, takes the detection route and either installs or fails as above; and a fabric manager the removal took before it stopped is not put back, because the reading cannot see what has gone. **What holds**: the fabric manager rule above for every one that still stands.
   - **On a container, never** ([R3](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of), [M2](#1-the-machine-kind-and-what-a-setup-can-be-on-each)). The driver is the host's, and the user is told instead (`Servers` H11b).
   - **How Smoozy tells a machine it can manage from one it cannot is what this document already holds, and nothing new.** It is the kind `Rented-server runtime` K1 detects — `systemd-detect-virt` plus the container marker file — read through [M2](#1-the-machine-kind-and-what-a-setup-can-be-on-each)'s table: on a systemd machine the driver is elevated work, and on a container it is never touched. **Whether the kind is a true stand-in for whether the driver can be changed is not established** ([Section 13](#13-unresolved) item 28).
   - **Debian is not established for this.** Its route installed 535.261.03 on Debian 12 (above), below 5.2's bar, and whether that family offers a newer branch is not read (item 28).
6. **Restart**, only where step 5's check failed after its work ([Section 8](#8-the-restart)); then the check again on return. This is Fact 80's rule as a step: restart only because the check failed, never on the package manager's word.
7. **Blender publication**, where the run fetched one: **the staged, unpacked build is asked its version in staging** under [B1](#5-the-blender-lifecycle)'s discipline and required to answer with the expected one, and **only on that answer is it renamed to its final folder** ([B8](#53-where-a-build-goes)). The order is the contract this document already cites for its own material — `Server protocol and transfers` §7.1 clause 3, *publication is the last step, and it is one step* ([B7](#52-a-build-the-user-points-at) clause 1) — and it is what makes the step re-runnable: the rename consumes the staged material, so a version ask placed after it would leave a re-run nothing to re-validate by content and a rename B8 forbids over an existing folder. Step 4 above already runs `ldd` over the staged build, so the staged build is exercised in place before it is published.
8. **Telegram material**, where Telegram is on ([Section 7](#7-the-telegram-roles-material-and-its-two-entry-points)).
9. **Done.** The final verification — `ldd` clean, the driver's own answer where a card is present, the published build answering — then the state cleared, which runs reconciliation (P5).

**R1a. A machine asked cold what cards it has can answer that it has none, and that answer is never read as a cardless machine.
