- **The interrupted job stays held back**, because [Y2](#31-two-classes-of-setup-run) holds it on that standing state and only the clear runs reconciliation (P5). **So a stopped machine-changing run reaches the same place [T6a](#8-the-restart) names for the user walking away, by a control rather than by neglect**, and the re-run is owed when a later run reaches *done*.
- **What the user meets on the next connection is [S1](#41-what-a-part-finished-setup-reports) and [S2](#41-what-a-part-finished-setup-reports)'s part-finished reading, and the route on is [S4](#42-the-recovery-run)'s recovery run** — which is Y8's *stopping leaves exactly what a lost connection leaves*, stated for the two things a lost connection also leaves standing.
- **An additive run sets no machine-level state ([Y1](#31-two-classes-of-setup-run)), so stopping one holds nothing back at all**: no admission changes and no job waits on it.

**Y9. One setup run at a time per machine, enforced at the supervisor's admission point, and declared in [Section 11](#11-declarations).** A second setup submission while one run is open is refused with the open run reported — Smoozy's own rule, per machine and never per app; several machines run at once. The refusal a *user's own* Blender can still produce — the add-on store lock — is [A8](#6-add-ons)'s and cannot be prevented from here. **The mechanism this is declared against had no field for it until 2026-08-22, and now has one.** `Rented-server runtime` A1 used to close an owning document's declarations at four, and the setup kind's class is *shared, no card* ([Section 11](#11-declarations)), which admits any number of setup runs — so this rule was declared against nothing. It was recorded as a seam at [Section 13](#13-unresolved) item 15, routed to the runner in [Section 12](#12-what-this-document-supplies-and-to-which-document), and **closed at the Architecture seam close by adding a fifth declaration** — machine-level exclusion of an operation's own kind, enforced at the admission point by counting admitted operations of that kind. The contended resources stay closed at the cards, the processors and the machine's memory, which is what stopped this being expressible before: making either shared resource exclusive would have excluded renders too, which is not what this rule says. [Section 11](#11-declarations)'s table now carries the exclusion as its last row. What held meanwhile — a machine-changing run held by the setup-in-progress state — still holds, and holds *every* kind rather than this one.

**The rule that acts on that declaration, and the refusal itself, now exist where the admission point does — added 2026-08-29 at the stage-close gate, and this clause asserts neither any longer.** `Rented-server runtime` **A5a** performs the fifth declaration as a numbered admission rule — *"An operation of a kind that declares machine-level exclusion of its own kind is not admitted while an operation of that kind is admitted on the machine"* — and **A5b** makes an operation A5a or A11 holds back **refused at the admission point rather than queued**, the refusal naming the operation standing in its way. **So this clause's *refused with the open run reported* is read out of the document that owns the admission point rather than promised from this end.** Until those two clauses, the declaration said only that the point counted, §7.2 never took it up, and every outcome that document named for an operation it would not admit was a **wait** — so a second submission would have queued unrefused and unreported, been admitted whenever the first run ended with the desktop that submitted it no longer driving it, and ended by [Y7](#33-the-worker-the-driving-and-the-stop)'s refresh window as *ended at boundary N, not completed*. **The refusal is narrow by A5b's own terms**, reaching A5a's exclusion and A11's state and nothing else: an operation waiting on a held resource still waits, so nothing here touches the render kind's queueing.

**Y9a. The installs still waiting behind a chain of Blender installs are a field of the server's row in the local record, so the ones a closed desktop never began are named when it opens again. Added 2026-10-16, Build step 10c's owed piece 4, on the build runner's ruling R3 (a hole under Rule 8); no auditor of this document has seen it.** Row 526's chain — several Blenders picked at once, each its own run of [Y6b](#32-the-recorded-safe-boundaries)'s kind, the next begun by the desktop when the last ends having installed its Blender, under one Y9 exclusion passed from one to the next — is driven by the desktop and held in its process memory. The machine sees one run at a time and never the list. So a desktop that exits before the last is begun leaves the rest unbegun, with nothing anywhere that knows they were asked for.

1. **What is held.** The versions picked and not yet begun, in the list's order, as `Servers` D3's row of 2026-10-16. **Written durably at the press** — the picks after the first; **rewritten when the chain begins the next** — the ones after it; **emptied when the chain ends**, however it ends, because every such ending already names on the card the ones it did not install. So a list standing while this application run drives no setup run on that server can only be one an earlier run left by exiting first.
2. **What reads it.** The Set up tab's reading at its opening ([B12](#54-the-search)'s first moment). A non-empty list, with no setup run driven by this run on the server, is drawn as Design's line. While a run is driven, nothing is drawn from it: the list is that run's own.
3. **What replaces it.** The next press on that server's card of the ready action, or of any Blender install — from the list, which keeps its own picks after the first, or the user's own build ([B7](#52-a-build-the-user-points-at)), which keeps nothing. A press refused by Y9 replaces nothing, since nothing began. **Nothing else writes it**, and it ends with the server row. *Added 2026-10-16, Build step 10c's owed piece 5:* **the ready action an *Add and set up* runs on its connection (`Servers` Q1 step 8) replaces it too, with nothing**, as *Set up again* does; it is the same act reached from the add rather than the card, and it is taken the same way. *No auditor of this document has seen it.*
4. **What it is not.** It is the user's own ask, recorded by the desktop that took it, and nothing read from a machine, so B12's period-held rule and Row 104 do not reach it. **Nothing resumes from it**: the installs it names are not begun on reopening, which is the safe side piece H of the Creator's third notes chose; the list only names them. A write that fails costs the line on a later opening and stops nothing.
5. **The one window it misses, accepted.** The list is rewritten once the next install is begun in the desktop's memory and before its run is asked of the machine, so a desktop that exits inside that window names one fewer than were never begun.

**Widened 2026-10-27: Y9a's chain is the desktop's line of setup acts on a server, not installs picked together.** The nineteenth notes, note 2, Creator-stated — a removal pressed while an install ran was refused at the desktop's own Y9 check and its line wiped from the card at once — with the build runner's rulings of that day under the Creator's standing delegation. *No auditor of this document has seen it.* Y9 is unchanged: the machine still admits one setup run at a time, and the line is what keeps a card press from ever meeting its refusal.

6. **What joins the line.** Every card act pressed while this desktop drives a setup run on that server is appended to the server's line instead of being refused: a Blender install from the list (each version picked is its own act, in the list's order), the user's own build ([B7](#52-a-build-the-user-points-at); the file is picked at the press and carried), a removal ([B15](#55-taking-a-blender-off); [B14](#55-taking-a-blender-off)'s covered set and the build's answered version composed at the press and carried, so the jobs marked are the jobs the warning named, `Queue & History` L21 unchanged), the ready action (*Set up again*; [W3](#10-what-this-module-says-about-a-machine)'s warning judged at the press, its render reading carried for T6a), and the Telegram placement ([G2](#7-the-telegram-roles-material-and-its-two-entry-points)). **The ready action is not among the running card's own acts**: the card does not offer it while a run goes and offers it again when the run ends; asked for from a place that still offers it during the run — the GPU-on-CPU line, the `Servers` row — it joins the line as above. *Changed 2026-10-30, Creator-stated, the twenty-fourth notes, note 1: "Set up again should not be available (grayed out or just non-existent) when setting up is actually happening. There's no point in this button in this scenario." Until then the running card offered it quietly after* Cancel setup. *No auditor of this document has seen it.* Each is begun as its own run of its own [Y6b](#32-the-recorded-safe-boundaries) kind, in the order pressed, when the run before it ends — the Y9 exclusion passed from one to the next under the one lock, never let go between them, so nothing else can be admitted between two acts of a line. The add's own setup (`Servers` Q1 step 8) and an abandon ([S6](#42-the-recovery-run)) are runs the line may stand behind; presses during them join it. **What is not built:** taking one act out of the line again.
7. **What begins the next act.** The run before it finished: *done*, and, for a Blender install from the list, its Blender published (Row 526's rule, unchanged); for the user's own build, *done* whether published or declined at [B7a](#52-a-build-the-user-points-at), the decline being the user's own answer and not a failure; for an abandon, the abandon performed. **Every other ending stops the line and begins nothing more**: a failed step, the connection lost, Smoozy's program not answering, and the user's stop ([Y8](#33-the-worker-the-driving-and-the-stop)). **So does a run that issued a restart** — `restart issued` ([Y3](#32-the-recorded-safe-boundaries)) among the boundaries its last reading reached, the ready action's item 6 or R1b's swap run — because a restarted machine is one the acts behind were not asked of; the ready action ending *done* after a restart still stops it. The acts not begun are named on the card after the ending's own sentence, in the order pressed.
8. **A run that does not drive the line.** R1b's swap run is begun by the desktop with no card press and lets its exclusion go by the plain ending; an act that joined during it is not begun, and the swap's ending names it as not begun. The swap always restarts the machine where it does anything, which is item 7's rule; where it ends without a restart the same naming holds, which is the narrower side and is accepted.
9. **The warnings are composed at the press.** W3's reading and B14's covered set are taken when the act is asked for, and are not taken again when its turn comes. **What breaks:** work that begins on the server between the press and the turn is not warned about — a render started there before a waiting ready action restarts the machine, or a job submitted with a build before its waiting removal. **What holds:** the warning shown was true at the press, the user saw it before deciding, and B14's mark covers exactly the set named. Accepted on the runner's ruling that the removal warning still shows at the press.
10. **What item 1 holds, from record version 54.** The acts not begun, in the order pressed, each as its kind and what it names — the version as the list asked for it, the build's file name, the removed build's answered version (or its path where none was read), and nothing for the ready action and the Telegram placement. The table keeps its name and adds the kind; every row before version 54 reads as a Blender install from the list. **Written durably at every change of the line**: at a press that joins it (the line with the act appended), when the next act begins (the acts after it), and emptied when the line ends however it ends. **Item 3 widens to every card act**: a press that begins a run replaces what an earlier run left, with nothing. A press that joins a line writes that line. Items 2, 4 and 5 stand, read as acts rather than versions.

---

## 4. Interruption, resumption, and the safe re-run

**Constraint:** Row 114 — *"Running a step again is always safe. Whether the last attempt finished, stopped part-way, or was never started, running it again puts the machine right rather than making it worse"* — with Fact 50 answered NO against a stub with no guard, and the spec's last sentence naming the escalation if the promise cannot be kept. **It is kept.** The negative was a run that was admitted while the state stood, started from the beginning, and cleared a state it did not own; each of those three is closed by construction below, and the per-step rules close what remains.

### 4.1 What a part-finished setup reports

**S1. Row 90: the machine is read for its setup state fresh, on the connection the user opens, and never from anything Smoozy remembered** ([Y6](#32-the-recorded-safe-boundaries); Row 104's rule applied to the setup state as much as to Blenders). The reading distinguishes *a step never started* from *a step that stopped part-way*: the boundary names the last step completed; the operation record and the journal name the step that was in flight and how the run ended, where it ended at all.

**S2. Row 112: *what it managed* is the machine's own record and has no second home.** The desktop holds its pending-command records (C3) and nothing else durable about a sitting — `State, identity and recovery` §1.1 is the bound, and this document adds no store to it. What the user is shown is composed from the fresh reading: the boundary list, the last one reached with its moment, and the run's ending where one was recorded. A machine that was recycled underneath reads as *absent* at the installation-identity gate and every remembered value is disqualified before any of this is read (§2.3) — the re-run this document defines is what the user meets there, on a machine that is genuinely bare again.

**S2a. The machine's own words for a step that failed are part of the run's recorded ending, on the machine, and are read back with the boundary list. Added 2026-08-25, per Row 490.** [Design](../Design/Server%20Setup.md) puts the failed step's raw output behind a control on a panel S1 and S2 compose from a fresh reading on a later connection — so the words must be durably on the machine, and nothing stated where, or under what term.

- **Where they live: the setup operation record's per-kind field, beside [Y4](#32-the-recorded-safe-boundaries)'s boundary list, written in the same durable act as the ending that names the failed step.** Not the event journal — its entries are structured at a 512-byte budget (`Rented-server runtime` §13 R2) and raw tool output is neither — and not §11's capture sinks, which are per render attempt and are not borrowed.
- **Who holds the words decides who writes them, and both routes end in the same field.** A machine-side step's words are the worker's own capture — [B1](#5-the-blender-lifecycle) already captures both streams of every Blender run this module makes — reported to the supervisor, which writes (`Rented-server runtime`'s workers-report-supervisor-writes discipline). An elevated step's process is the connection's child ([Y7](#33-the-worker-the-driving-and-the-stop)), so its streams land in the desktop's own loop and nowhere on the machine; the desktop, which classifies the failure with those words in hand ([E4](#9-elevation-the-refusal-and-the-password)), writes the ending with the words through the ordinary command path — the same route that writes the stop's durable state ([Y8](#33-the-worker-the-driving-and-the-stop)). Where the failure is Smoozy's own verification rather than a tool's refusal — a digest that did not match, an `ldd` re-read that did not come back clean, a version answer that was not the expected one — the recorded fact of that read is the ending's content, and no tool words are claimed for it.
- **Verbatim, whole or not at all, streams kept apart** — `Security and licence` §13 rule 5 and `Rented-server runtime` M7 and M8's discipline, cited rather than restated: never mined for a cause, never merged, never trimmed. A write the state area cannot take whole retains no fragment; the ending is recorded either way, saying the words were not kept — **the ending never waits on the words.**
- **A step cut mid-flight has no words anywhere, and none are claimed.** The elevated process died with its channel and its printed text with the desktop's loop; the run's ending is then the kind's own *ended at boundary N, not completed*, written when [Y7](#33-the-worker-the-driving-and-the-stop)'s window elapses or at [S4](#42-the-recovery-run)'s take-over, carrying no words. **The two cases are told apart by the ending's own kind and never by the words' absence**: a step that failed is an ending that names it, with the words beside it where any were printed; a run that was cut is that other ending. The design's control has something to name on the first and nothing on the second, and the drawing is Design's.
- **Retention is the record's own and nothing new is retained**: the words ride the ready-action record and end when [Y6b](#32-the-recorded-safe-boundaries)'s per-kind term replaces it at the next run of its kind — which is the design's own lifecycle for this panel, *the run is over, and the next run writes a new list*.

**S3. Row 106: *carries on where it left off* is a resumption from the recorded boundary, driven over the re-made connection.** Nothing on the machine resumes by itself: reconciliation is held by the state (step 0), and the machine's only autonomous act is holding everything back. The resumption is [S4](#42-the-recovery-run) item 2's recovery run, issued by the desktop when the machine answers again after a restart Smoozy caused ([T5](#8-the-restart)) or when the user runs the setup again.

### 4.2 The recovery run

**S4. Running it again is safe because a second run can neither start beside the first nor start from the beginning.**

1. **Admission is the guard Fact 50's stub lacked.** While the machine-level state is set, a fresh setup submission is **refused at the admission point and not queued (A5b)** — the hold-back being P2 and A11's, with [Y1](#31-two-classes-of-setup-run)'s only-the-named-run exception (A11a) — and the refusal names **the operation the standing state holds (A5b, on the identity P1 carries)**, the **boundary and its moment** being the reading [Y6](#32-the-recorded-safe-boundaries) already names. No error, no silent second worker, no two runs in flight. **Two citation corrections, both 2026-08-29 at the stage-close gate, and the refusal itself does not move.** This item cited **P8** for the whole report. P8 carries the state and its identity and **names no boundary** — *"It reports the state and its identity, and what happens next is `Server Setup`'s safe re-run"* — so the boundary list and its last-reached marker, each with its moment on the machine's clock ([Y3](#32-the-recorded-safe-boundaries)), are [Y6](#32-the-recorded-safe-boundaries)'s reading and the health answer's (V9). And **P8's actor is a setup run that finds the state already set, which is precisely what P2 and A11a never admit**; the actor is A5b's admission point, which issues the refusal to the submitter and names the operation identity the state holds. **What this item asserted it now reads out of the document that owns the admission point**: before A5a and A5b, *refused* was stated here and nowhere else, and every outcome that document named for an operation it would not admit was a **wait**.
2. **The route forward is the recovery run, and it takes the standing state over rather than clearing it.** A recovery command — an ordinary command with its own operation identity, naming the setup operation the state names — has the supervisor: validate the recorded group; write the named operation's recorded outcome (*left stopped* from its durable stop, or the kind's own *ended at boundary N*); **re-point the machine-level state at the recovery run's own operation identity, and in that same durable act write the composed boundary list and the last-reached marker into the recovery run's *own* operation record's per-kind field**; and start the recovery run's worker. **Where the marker is written matters and *preserving* is not enough on its own**: a boundary lives in the setup operation record's per-kind field ([Y3](#32-the-recorded-safe-boundaries)) and not in the machine-level state, and the take-over writes the old operation's recorded outcome — which closes that record — and opens a new one. Written into the recovery's own record, the recovery begins at the boundary it inherited, and a second interruption before it writes a boundary of its own still resumes from that inherited one rather than from the beginning. The state is never cleared in between, so no admission window ever opens — **clearing first and starting fresh would run reconciliation (P5) and start the interrupted job while the machine is still being changed underneath it, which is Row 110's forbidden outcome; the take-over is required, not stylistic.**
3. **The recovery run resumes at the recorded boundary and never before it.** Steps at or before the marker are not run again; the step after it runs afresh under its own re-run rule below.
4. **What the inherited steps read is inherited with the boundary, and the steps driven are the taken-over run's. Added 2026-09-22 at Build, found by step 10c's third pass on Vast.** *Fetched* guarantees an archive *"whole at its staging path"* and *tooling* one *"unpacked into staging"* ([Y4](#32-the-recorded-safe-boundaries)), and the unpack and R1 item 7's publication read the Blender version. **A take-over that carried the marker and not these resumed at a step that could never find what the marker promises.** So the take-over's durable act carries two more values from the taken-over record into the recovery's own: **the Blender version the run was commanded with**, which the machine records in the per-kind field from the first step that carries one, and **the name its staged material sits under** — the run that fetched, carried unchanged down a chain of recoveries. A build the taken-over run unpacked is found again where the unpack put it. **And the recovery drives the taken-over run's remaining steps, in its inherited list, not the steps of whatever act opened it** — [Y6b](#32-the-recorded-safe-boundaries) already gives the recovery that run's kind. On the third pass *Set up this server* took over a Blender install stopped after *fetched*, drove only its own *done*, and wrote it: the install's unpack, libraries and publication were dropped without a word and the screen said the server was ready to render with no Blender on it. [Y3](#32-the-recorded-safe-boundaries)'s own meaning now refuses that at the machine as well: see the dated note there.
5. **Which run is taken over where no machine-level state stands, and that it is taken over at all. Added 2026-09-22 at Build, found by step 10c's fourth pass on Vast.** Item 1 reads the state's refusal as what sends the press down this route, and an **additive** run sets no state ([Y1](#31-two-classes-of-setup-run)) — so a Blender install stopped at a boundary on a machine that already held the unpack tool was refused by nothing, *Set up again* opened a fresh ready action, and the stopped run was forgotten with the archive it had fetched still on the disk. **[S3](#4-interruption-resumption-and-the-safe-re-run)'s *or when the user runs the setup again* covers that case and always did; what was missing was a way to name the run.** So: **where no state stands, the run the press takes over is the latest setup run of any kind the desktop knows it drove on that server whose own record says it did not finish** — still open, ended at its boundary, left stopped, or a step failed. A run that reached *done*, was abandoned, or was declined at the version answer is finished business and the press opens a fresh run. **The reading is the machine's own record** ([S1](#41-what-a-part-finished-setup-reports), [S2](#41-what-a-part-finished-setup-reports)) and the desktop holds only the identity, which is no second home for anything. **Nothing about the state changes**: the take-over re-points a state only where one was there to re-point, and setting one for an additive run's recovery would hold reconciliation and every admission back on a machine nothing was changing, which [Y1](#31-two-classes-of-setup-run) says at length is not the safe direction. **What this cannot reach is stated rather than hidden:** a stopped additive run driven before this copy of Smoozy was opened is not found, because no request answers a setup record by its kind and the machine's store enumerates only open records — the same limit [Y6a](#32-the-recorded-safe-boundaries) already carries, and its staged material is [Section 11](#11-declarations)'s sweep's.

**S5. A recorded group that still validates refuses the recovery, and that is the safe direction of the one reading known to be wrong.** The defunct-entry hole means a dead setup group can read as live until the next supervisor start; it can never read as dead while live (`Rented-server runtime` §16 item 4 — X9's other direction holds). So a recovery refused because the pair validates costs a wait, never a second run beside a live one. **What unblocks it, said exactly, because two of the three things once named here do not.** The unblock that holds in the ordinary case is **the group genuinely ending** — the worker ends its run at a boundary under [Y7](#33-the-worker-the-driving-and-the-stop). A **machine restart on the systemd kind** also reaps, and [T5](#8-the-restart) is what follows one; but this module never issues a restart to unblock this, because [T1](#8-the-restart) issues one only as part of a setup step that needs it and the setup run is precisely what is being refused, and on a container no restart is issued at all (M2, T1, Fact 48). **A supervisor restart is not carried as an unblock at all**: nothing in any document this one may read establishes that a supervisor restart reaps the workers it started — what was recorded is the opposite, that *the supervisor never reaps the workers it starts* (`Rented-server runtime` §16 item 4's rider, **closed 2026-08-22 by X9a's wait**, which is a wait and not a restart: *"§3.2's one-restarter rule is untouched — a wait is not a restart"*) — and the one reap actually observed was a reboot. [Section 9b](#9b-the-user-facing-fallback-re-run) F1 step 3's plain start does not reach it either: that step fires only where the supervisor does **not** answer, and here it answers.

**The live-supervisor case had no route out until 2026-08-22; the route is the reap, and `Rented-server runtime` X9a now requires it.** What the refusal costs without it is the record below. With a defunct pair reading as live on a machine whose supervisor is up, nothing this document defines clears the state; nothing is admitted at machine level while it stands (P2, A11) — no render, no reading, no derivative, no re-run of the interrupted job — and the acts that would end it are outside the app: a reboot from the provider's interface, which *servers and keys only, never providers* forbids ([M4](#1-the-machine-kind-and-what-a-setup-can-be-on-each)) and which Row 283's exception exists to prevent, or destroying the rental. **The route out is the reap itself, and this document carries it as its own requirement rather than as somebody else's parked item**: the supervisor must reap the workers it starts, routed to the runner in [Section 12](#12-what-this-document-supplies-and-to-which-document) with what breaks at [Section 13](#13-unresolved) item 16, and **no longer Build's**: `Rented-server runtime` X9a states the wait from that document's side. **Nothing here relies on the liveness reading being right, and nothing softens the refusal** ([Section 14](#14-moments-that-reached-the-creator) records the check on what this costs the user).

**S6. Only two things clear the state: a run reaching *done*, and a recovery run the user asked to abandon the machine-changing work — which still runs the final verification first and reports what it found.** Both clears run reconciliation (P5). Nothing else clears it, on the strength of nothing (P7).

**S5's wait is built, for the abandon and for a recovery alike — added 2026-09-22 at Build.** A recovery refused because the run it would take over is still running — the supervisor still holds that run open, or its recorded group still validates under `Rented-server runtime` A5a — is refused **before anything is taken over**, and the refusal says so in a field of its own; the desktop waits at [Y7](#33-the-worker-the-driving-and-the-stop)'s cadence and asks again, until it is admitted, the user stops, or a bound the desktop names passes, and past the bound the refusal is shown in the machine's words. **Refused before the take-over and not after it**, because a take-over closes the old record and re-points the state, and a recovery whose worker was then refused would leave a state naming a run that never ran and nothing a second try could name. The wire's words and the bound are Build's and are recorded in its decisions record. This is `Design/Server Setup § The two ways out`'s *"either waits if the server is still finishing the stopped run"*.

**What the abandon is, run by run — added 2026-09-22 at Build, which had not built it; step 10c's third pass met a driver step that failed the same way on every *Set up again*, and a user with no way on but destroying the server.** It is [S4](#42-the-recovery-run) item 2's recovery command, taking the standing state over exactly as any recovery does, and marked as an abandon. **It drives [R1](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of) item 9's final verification and nothing else, and writes no boundary.** Its record ends with the kind's own ending *abandoned* whatever the verification found, carrying the verification's words where it did not pass ([S2a](#41-what-a-part-finished-setup-reports)'s field) — the finding is what the abandon reports, never a step it failed. **The clear is then `Rented-server runtime` P3's second act, the same request a run reaching *done* sends**, and it runs reconciliation (P5). **Where no state stands there is nothing to abandon and nothing is sent.** The wire's words are Build's and are recorded in its decisions record.

### 4.3 Why each step is safe to run again

**S7. Per step, with the mechanism named. One rule spans every step below that installs a package — the tooling step, the libraries step and the driver route on either family alike: an install interrupted mid-transaction can leave the machine's package system needing its own repair first, and that step's re-run begins with the repair operation** where the package system reports itself interrupted. **Widened 2026-09-04, out of the round-1 code audit of this module's build; the enumeration read *the tooling step and the libraries step alike*.** When M1b was added on 2026-08-22 the Debian driver route became a package install of its own, with a list update before it, and the Ubuntu route drives the same package system — so the driver step had no repair precondition and its re-run walked straight into a package system needing repair. **S7b already says the wider thing** of the bounded wait — *every package step of a setup run* — so two clauses of one section instructed differently about one set of steps. S7b is right as it stands and is not touched.

**The repair operation is `dpkg --configure -a` followed by `apt-get --fix-broken install`, and nothing forced.** It is the repair the machine itself prints in exactly the situation this rule fires on — apt's own source, `apt-pkg/deb/debsystem.cc`, read 2026-08-20 at `https://salsa.debian.org/apt-team/apt/-/raw/main/apt-pkg/deb/debsystem.cc`: `cmd = "dpkg --configure -a";` … `_error->Error(_("dpkg was interrupted, you must manually run '%s' to correct the problem. "), cmd)` — and it is dpkg's own documented one, `dpkg(1)`, read 2026-08-23 at `https://manpages.debian.org/trixie/dpkg/dpkg.1.en.html` (**the four quotations below carried a read date of 2026-08-20 and no address; the manual was read at that address on 2026-08-23 and all four hold word for word**): *"Configure a package which has been unpacked but not yet configured. If -a or --pending is given instead of package, all unpacked but unconfigured packages are configured."* **`dpkg --remove --force-all` is forbidden as the first act of any re-run.** It was recorded once as an observed recovery (Fact 48's container case) and written here as the rule; dpkg's own manual is why it cannot be: *"These options are mostly intended to be used by experts only. Using them without fully understanding their effects may break your whole system."*, and `all` turns on `remove-essential` — *"Removing them might cause the whole system to stop working"* — and `remove-protected` — *"Removing them might cause the whole system to be unable to boot"*. A forced removal opening every re-run sits directly against Row 114's promise that running the action again puts the machine right rather than making it worse.

**S7b. A package step distinguishes *busy* from *broken* before it repairs anything, and this is the ordinary case rather than the rare one.** Ubuntu installs `unattended-upgrades` by default and runs it at startup — Ubuntu Server documentation, *Automatic updates*, read 2026-08-20 at `https://ubuntu.com/server/docs/how-to/software/automatic-updates/`: *"unattended-upgrades may often run on system startup and thereby cause immediate activity and prevent other package operations from taking place at that time"*, and it names the counter-productive case as *"VM images that are only started for some quick action"*, which is a rented render box. [The rented machine](The%20rented%20machine.md) §5 records `unattended-upgrades.service loaded active running` on a real rental and §9 D an unasked kernel upgrade. **A package operation blocked by the frontend lock fails rather than waits**: apt's `DPkg::Lock::Timeout` defaults to `0` and its lock acquisition returns immediately at that value (apt's own source, same address), and the refusal reads *"Unable to acquire the dpkg frontend lock (%s), is another process using it?"*. **So every package step of a setup run sets `DPkg::Lock::Timeout` to a bounded wait rather than accepting the default**, and a step that still cannot take the lock is reported failed as *the machine is busy with its own updates* — never repaired, because nothing is broken. Without this, a step that failed only because the machine was busy re-runs into the repair operation against a package system that was never damaged. **The bounded wait's length is not established** and is [Section 13](#13-unresolved) item 19; the setting is documented in apt's source and in neither `apt.conf(5)` nor `apt-get(8)`, which is itself the reason the value wants observing rather than reading.

**S7c. The bounded wait was exercised at boot for the first time on 2026-08-22, and what it did not exercise is the collision it exists for.** [The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20%E2%80%94%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md), on a Debian virtual machine **at 108 seconds of uptime** — the race this document had never posed: `apt-get -o DPkg::Lock::Timeout=120 update` returned **0 in 31 seconds**, fetching 26.7 MB, and the frontend lock was reported **not held** at that moment. **So the route works and the wait was not needed.** **The negative would have been the documented sequence failing to clear the lock, or waiting longer than a setup reasonably can**, and neither happened. **What this does not establish is the loaded case:** the automatic-update timers were enabled on that image and the nearer one was **17 minutes away**, so the run did not collide with a job actually holding the lock. **Item 19's value is therefore still unobserved** — what was observed is that the setting is accepted and the ordinary boot does not collide.

**S7d. Every package install of a setup run, on either family, is preceded by a refresh of the machine's package lists. Added 2026-09-22, found at Build by step 10c's third pass.** An install is resolved against the lists the machine already holds, and a rented image's lists are as old as the image. apt's own manual, `apt-get(8)` (APT for Debian trixie, `https://manpages.debian.org/trixie/apt/apt-get.8.en.html`, read 2026-09-22): *"update is used to resynchronize the package index files from their sources."* Where a package named by stale lists is no longer in the archive, the install fails, and apt's own source says what it asks for — `apt-private/private-install.cc`, `https://salsa.debian.org/apt-team/apt/-/raw/main/apt-private/private-install.cc`, read 2026-09-22: *"Unable to fetch some archives, maybe run apt update or try with --fix-missing?"* **Observed**: Hyperstack's plain `Ubuntu Server 24.04 LTS` image carried lists whose newest file was dated 2025-06-15; [R1](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of) item 5's install of `ubuntu-drivers-common` fetched the version those lists named and the archive answered *"404  Not Found"*, then apt printed the same sentence in its `apt-get` form (label: **observed in a test**, one image; `Audits/Build/Step 10c/Piece 12, the third pass — Hyperstack.md`). **Until this clause the refresh was M1b's Debian components step alone**, which updates the lists only because it has just enabled new components; nothing refreshed them in front of any other install on either family. **The refresh is `apt-get -o DPkg::Lock::Timeout=120 update`**, elevated, under S7b's bounded wait like every package step, and it is issued in the same elevated form as the install it precedes, directly before it. **Where it does not end cleanly the step ends there in the machine's own words and the install is not sent** — against lists that could not be refreshed it would be the install that failed. It takes the class of the install it precedes ([Y1](#31-two-classes-of-setup-run)): it is part of that act, and the state is set before either goes out. **The class, every install either route makes:**

- **The unpack tool** (`xz-utils`, R1 item 3), either family — **refreshed**.
- **The libraries** (R1 item 4), either family — **refreshed**.
- **The Ubuntu detection tool** (`ubuntu-drivers-common`, M1b) — **refreshed**.
- **The Debian driver** (`nvidia-driver`, M1b) — **refreshed**; where the components step has just enabled the components, the lists are then refreshed twice, and M1b's own refresh stays where M1b puts it.
- **The Ubuntu driver install** (`ubuntu-drivers install --gpgpu`, M1b) — **not refreshed again**: it is not an `apt-get` command, and it is issued in the same step directly after the tool install, whose refresh it runs against.
- **The repair** (`apt-get --fix-broken install`, S7) — **not refreshed**: no route issues it, and whether a refresh may go in front of the repair of an interrupted transaction is not read. Whoever first issues the repair decides it.

**What it costs** is one refresh per install — at most four in one run on Debian, three on Ubuntu. **What it does not establish** is that a refreshed install succeeds: a mirror that is down still fails the refresh, and the step then says so in the machine's own words rather than failing on the install.

Then, per step:

- **fetch** — **not a `Server protocol and transfers` §7 transfer, and the mechanism is not borrowed.** [B4](#51-the-list-and-the-fetch) puts this fetch on the machine, over the machine's own line, between the machine and Blender's downloads: the bytes never pass through Smoozy's own byte loop, and that loop is where §7's checkpoint digests come from (S22 — *"there is no second loop on the machine for this digest to be computed in"*), with the checkpoint record itself kept per transfer on the desktop side (S9, S12). Neither exists here, so §7's prefix verification is not available and is not claimed. What binds is §7.1's four contract clauses, cited rather than restated: part-arrived bytes never at a final name, integrity measured against staged material, publication last, abandoned staging swept. What that comes to for this fetch: the archive is staged and never at a final name; the whole is verified against the published checksum before anything is unpacked; **a resume is by byte range into the same staging path** — the downloads honour byte ranges at `http_code=206` (B4) — with the published checksum as the only verification, a whole-file checksum being unable to verify a prefix; and on a mismatch the staged material is abandoned and the fetch starts over into fresh staging, never repaired in place (S24). Either way nothing half-fetched is ever installed.
- **tooling / unpack** — an install cut off part-way was run again over the same folder with nothing deleted first and produced a working Blender the same as a clean one — **observed**, one archive, one machine, one time (*Builds 9*; the row file's own quotation). The unpack is into staging, so even that case is narrower here than the observation needed. **That observation covers the unpack half alone.** The step's other half is the unpack tool installed where absent, which is an elevated package install and can be cut mid-transaction exactly as the libraries step's can ([Y7](#33-the-worker-the-driving-and-the-stop): a connection lost mid-step kills the step mid-flight) — *Builds 9* says nothing about a package system, and it is the repair precondition above that covers it.
- **libraries** — the package installs are re-issued as the same named list; a package already installed is not work repeated. The package-system repair precondition above applies to this step in full.
- **driver** — the re-run is the same check-first order as the first run ([R1](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of) item 5): a driver that answers is never touched, so the reinstall-becomes-upgrade harm is unreachable from a re-run by construction; a driver that does not answer gets the restart before any second install, which is the boot both observed mechanisms wanted.
- **restart** — idempotent in effect; a second restart costs its return time and nothing else (Fact 51). **A recovery resuming at the restart step re-runs step 5's check first and issues the restart only where it fails again.** Whether a restart is owed is a reading of the machine now and never an outcome recorded in a previous sitting: Row 104's rule is that what a machine holds is read afresh, and `State, identity and recovery` D2 forbids inferring what to do from a stale observed reading. Between the cut and the reconnection a machine can reboot for a reason of its own — the register records a box that installs its own updates unasked — and the driver then answers, so the recorded outcome would have Smoozy restart a paid machine at 9 to 90 seconds for a step no longer owed. The check is one `nvidia-smi` invocation and is free of consequence ([R1](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of) item 5's check-first order, [R5](#22-what-the-action-never-does)); the *driver placed* boundary's recorded outcome is a record of what happened, not an instruction for the next sitting.
- **blender publication** — publication is one rename, and a re-run re-validates staged material by content before reusing a byte (G19's rule applied to this document's material).
- **telegram material** — the placement is file placement plus a desired-state write, both idempotent under C4; replacement deliveries reuse the same vehicle (`Telegram` L4).

**What the mechanism costs**: the boundary records and their verification reads (a read per step), the admission refusal and its report, and the recovery command — no user-facing cost the spec does not already grant, and no second store anywhere. **The promise is kept; `Method`'s first escalation is not reached.** [Section 14](#14-moments-that-reached-the-creator) records the check.

---

## 5. The Blender lifecycle

### 5.1 The list and the fetch

**Constraint:** Row 92, and the Creator's rulings of 2026-08-12: the list is built from the shop-front, the fetch stays on Blender's own downloads, and the quick path installs the newest finished release, whichever line publishes it, without preferring an older long-term-support release (Creator-stated 2026-10-27). **Revised by the Creator on 2026-10-02 for unfinished builds alone**: alpha, beta and release-candidate builds are listed and fetched from Blender's own build service ([B2](#51-the-list-and-the-fetch), [B4](#51-the-list-and-the-fetch)). The fetch still comes only from Blender itself.

**B1 comes first — the reading discipline every Blender run in this document sits inside (Row 94).** A build is asked its version by running it with its version switch; **the answer is the line beginning `Blender `, never the first line** — the apt build printed two lines of colour-management noise first (Fact 45). **That same line carries the other two marks Row 94 promises, and both are taken from it and from nothing else**: whether the build is a long-term-support release (**observed**) and whether it is a finished release or an unstable build (**documented**, and untested by this project, every build tested having been a finished release). No folder name, path or channel is parsed for either — what a machine holds is read by asking the build, and the listings' own marks ([B2](#51-the-list-and-the-fetch)) describe the list rather than a build on a machine. Both streams are captured, always — the refusal to open a window goes to the error stream in 4.5 and the ordinary output in the other three, and some of Blender's own messages reach the error stream in ordinary runs anyway; **an empty error stream is never read as a property of having no window** (*Running Blender with nobody there*). **Every Blender run this module makes carries a time limit and something outside Blender ready to kill it** — *"Anything running 4.5 unattended needs a time limit and something outside Blender ready to kill it; the other three versions do not"* — applied to all four versions because the module cannot know a candidate's version before asking. **What that kill reaches is stated here, because two rules elsewhere forbid every signal and neither is meant to reach this one.** The limit's kill is a bound on one invocation the setup run made, never the setup kind's termination: **the target is the invoked Blender process and nothing above it** — never the setup worker, never a shell around it, never anything found by name, pattern or process search — which is the shape `Rendering` already takes from `Rented-server runtime` X12 for a render. [Y8](#33-the-worker-the-driving-and-the-stop)'s rule that no signal is ever sent to the worker or its children, and [Section 11](#11-declarations)'s *none* for which member receives a termination, are both about ending the **operation**, and neither reaches a child the run itself put a limit on; the package system mid-transaction is still never signalled, and this narrows those rules' reach rather than weakening them. A run killed at its limit answers nothing and is recorded as answering nothing — **named as one that did not answer** where it was a search candidate ([B11](#54-the-search)). **And no build's engine list is got by the command that prints one, on an unattended machine** — two topics record that command both working and failing with no display server, both stand, and *"Until it is settled, do not get a build's engine list that way on an unattended machine."* Nothing in this document asks for an engine list.

**B2. The list is read from two machine-readable listings — finished releases from the shop-front's `stable` channels, unfinished builds from Blender's own build service — and sorted by parsed version and never by string.** *Rewritten 2026-10-02 on the Creator's revision of that day (see the constraint above); the version that stood until then, which offered the shop-front's own unfinished channels, is in git.* **The shop-front is asked once more, at once, where its first answer is no use. Added 2026-11-03, a hole on the second Sesterce pass: the first *Install Blender…* box read that the list could not be read, with the line answering, and a second opening read it. What the first ask met was not caught, so this is a retry and not a cause.** No auditor has seen it.

- **Finished releases.** Observed, 2026-08-19 (see [Sources](Sources/Server%20Setup.sources.md)): the Snap Store's info endpoint for the Blender Foundation's own package answers one entry per channel, each carrying the track (`latest`, `4.5lts`, `5.0`, …), the risk (`stable`, `candidate`, `beta`, `edge`), the version, the release moment and the byte size — the long-term-support lines marked in the track's own name. **Only channels whose risk is `stable` are offered**, at or above the whole-app floor of 4.5. The shop-front's other risks are not offered: none of them ever had a file Smoozy could fetch ([B5](#51-the-list-and-the-fetch)).
- **Unfinished builds.** Observed, 2026-10-02: Blender's own build service, `https://builder.blender.org/download/daily/?format=json&v=1`, answers one entry per file, each carrying `version`, `risk_id`, `platform`, `architecture`, `file_name`, `file_mtime` and more ([B6](#51-the-list-and-the-fetch)). **An entry is offered where its `platform` is `linux`, its `architecture` is `x86_64`, its `file_name` ends `-linux.x86_64-release.tar.xz`, and its `risk_id` is not `stable`** — at or above 4.5. **Of entries with the same version and the same `risk_id`, only the one with the newest `file_mtime` is offered**, which is what disarms B6's stale-entry trap. Nothing else about an entry is judged. **Each keeps its `risk_id` as its kind**; the words the user reads for it are Design's.
- **One narrowing over both, Creator-stated 2026-09-27 and widened 2026-09-28: an unfinished build is not offered where a `stable` channel in the shop-front's answer carries the same major and minor version at the same or a newer version, compared as parsed version components.** So a release candidate at 5.2.2 goes beside a `stable` 5.2.2, a beta at 4.5.0 goes beside a `stable` 4.5.14, and an alpha 5.3.0 stays while no `stable` channel carries 5.3. **The comparison is within one series (major and minor), never across the list**: the question put to the Creator was about a line that already has a newer full release, and a fix build of an older long-term-support series is not made irrelevant by a newer series. **What it cannot reach, stated rather than guessed around:** the shop-front's answer carries each channel's current version only, so an unfinished build of a series that no `stable` channel in that answer carries is still offered, even where that series was released once; and an entry that could not be read narrows nothing.
- **A second narrowing, over finished releases alone: of the offered `stable` channels whose versions share a major and minor, only the highest, compared as parsed version components, is offered.** So where `latest/stable` carries 5.2.2 and `5.2lts/stable` carries 5.2.3, the list offers 5.2.3 and not 5.2.2; a series with one `stable` channel is unchanged, and 4.5's newest release stands beside a newer series. **It is judged from the listing's offered entries and never from the builds on the machine** (B6a's version mark is untouched), so installing a version never changes what is offered. Two channels carrying the same version are still one version, as Design draws them. It is applied before the narrowing above, which reads the same `stable` channels and is not changed by it. B3's choice is unchanged, since the highest of a series is the one this keeps. *Added 2026-10-28, Creator-stated, the twentieth notes, note 1, with the smallest change runner-ruled under the Creator's standing delegation; carried down from `Specs/Server Setup`. No auditor has seen it.*
- **An entry the floor rules out is not an entry that could not be read. Added 2026-11-02, the first 5.3 live pass, which saw *3 entries could not be read*; no auditor of this document has seen it.** Read from the Creator's Mac that day: the shop-front's answer held 41 entries and those 3 were `2.82/stable` at `2.82a`, `2.81/stable` at `2.81a` and `2.79/stable` at `2.79b`; the build service gave none. Label: **observed**. Their versions end in a letter, so they are not version components, and they are far below the floor, so B2 would never offer them. **So a version that is numbers followed by letters is placed against the floor by its numbers. Below the floor it is simply not offered and not counted, like every other entry below it. At or above the floor it is still named**, because a supported version with a letter is a real shortfall. Everything else unreadable is named as before. **What the count then says is true: entries that could have been offered and were not.**
- **Every value read is the listing's own field**, so nothing is read off a track's name, a file's name or a version's spelling, except the `file_name` ending above, which selects the one archive form B4 fetches. A plain alphabetical sort picks 4.2.9 as newest where 4.2.23 exists (Fact 54), so the sort is on version components. How finished releases and unfinished builds are told apart in the list is Design's, and both listings carry the marks it needs.

**B3. *The latest finished release* is the highest version among B2's offered `stable` channels, compared as parsed major, minor and patch numbers, whichever track publishes it.** **Observed 2026-10-27:** `latest/stable` named 5.2.2 while `5.2lts/stable` named 5.2.3; `latest/candidate` also named 5.2.3. The default track therefore does not decide which finished release is newest (see [Sources](Sources/Server%20Setup.sources.md), *The shop-front read again, 2026-10-27*). No unfinished channel can win, and no older long-term-support release is preferred. *Changed 2026-10-27, the nineteenth notes, note 1; no auditor has seen it.*

**B4. The fetch derives the folder from the version and stays on Blender's own downloads.** `Blender<major.minor>/blender-<version>-linux-x64.tar.xz` under the release listing, with the checksum file `blender-<version>.sha256` beside it in the same folder — observed 2026-08-19 for 5.2.0, 5.1.2, 5.0.1, 4.5.0 through 4.5.12, 4.3.1 and 4.2.20 through 4.2.23 (see [Sources](Sources/Server%20Setup.sources.md)). The fetch runs **on the machine**, over the machine's own line (Fact 54: 37 MB/s on the machine's own line, against **1.2–1.3 MB/s up** from the user's connection — Fact 25, `Six machines`, one home line on one evening, and **0.62 MB/s up** on the later reading of that line, `Server protocol and transfers` R17, 2026-08-22. **Corrected 2026-08-29**: the uplink figure read *1.2 MB/s* and was cited to Fact 54, which carries the machine-side figure and no uplink figure at all; both figures now stand under the fact that carries them and with that reading's own bound. **The clause is unchanged in substance and stronger for it** — at the later reading the machine's line is sixty times the user's rather than thirty), staged and resumable — the downloads honour byte ranges at `http_code=206` — and verified against the published checksum before anything is unpacked.

**An unfinished build is fetched from the build service, and the machine is never handed an address. Added 2026-10-02 with B2's revision.** The install step carries, beside the version, which listing the build came from and, for the build service, the entry's `file_name`. **The machine composes the address from a second constant base, `https://cdn.builder.blender.org/download/daily/`, and that name** — the base the build service's own listing gives every entry (observed 2026-10-02). **A name holding a `/`, or not ending `-linux.x86_64-release.tar.xz`, is refused before any request**: a base that arrives from outside is what this clause's constant base exists to forbid, and a bare name under a constant base is not one. **The checksum is read at `<the archive's address>.sha256`, and its body is a bare digest** — one line, no file name — observed 2026-10-02, which is not the per-file shape of a release folder's `blender-<version>.sha256`. A body of any other shape is *the published checksum could not be read*, as on the release route. Byte ranges are honoured there too (`206`, observed 2026-10-02), so the fetch is staged and resumable on the same terms. Everything after the fetch — the unpack, B1's version ask, [R1](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of) item 7's expected answer, B8's publication — is the release route's, unchanged. See [Sources](Sources/Server%20Setup.sources.md).

**B5. Fact 52, read twice: Blender's release folders hold finished releases only, so the shop-front's unfinished channels named builds Smoozy could never fetch.** On 2026-08-19 every `stable` channel had its exact file at the derived address, while the `candidate` and `edge` channels named 5.2.1 (404) and 5.3.0 (no release folder). On 2026-10-02 the same held: `latest/edge` named 5.3.0, `Blender5.3/` answered 404, and `Blender5.2/` held 5.2.0 to 5.2.2 and nothing named alpha, beta or rc. **This clause read that as a case that *can* happen; on both days it was every unfinished entry**, and the Creator's 2026-10-02 note 7 met it. **Resolved by [B2](#51-the-list-and-the-fetch)'s revision**: unfinished builds now come from the build service, which lists the same 5.3.0 as an alpha with a file and a checksum beside it. **What can still happen, and the promise for it stands as written**: a build the list names whose file is gone by the time the machine asks — an alpha rebuilt on the build service between the list and the fetch (*expected*: the old name answers 404 once replaced), or a finished release the shop-front names before its folder exists. **Smoozy says the build could not be fetched and names it, by version and kind**, and never quietly substitutes a different version. Whether installing through a named shop-front channel delivers that release was never run, and nothing installs through the shop-front. Label: **observed**, two readings, [Sources](Sources/Server%20Setup.sources.md).

**B6. Fact 53 is posed by reading, 2026-08-19: blender.org's own build service publishes a machine-readable listing, and daily builds sit on it.** The builder's daily listing answers as structured data, one entry per artefact, carrying version, risk (`alpha`, `candidate`, `stable`), branch, hash, platform, architecture, size and file moment (see [Sources](Sources/Server%20Setup.sources.md)). ~~Nothing in this document builds on it — the ruling fixes the list's source.~~ **From 2026-10-02, [B2](#51-the-list-and-the-fetch) lists unfinished builds from it and [B4](#51-the-list-and-the-fetch) fetches them from it**, on the Creator's revision of that day; read again that day, it answered the same shape. One trap carried: **the listing held one entry two years stale beside the current ones**, so anything reading it reads the file moment and does not assume every entry current — which B2's newest-per-version-and-kind rule does.

**B6a. What the offered list matches against on the machine, and what the mark may claim. Added 2026-08-21, per Row 363.** [Design](../Design/Server%20Setup.md) says the list says *"which of them are already on this server"*, and B2's entries and B10's findings are not the same kind of thing.

- **A published entry is a channel or a listed file, not a build on this machine.** B2's shop-front entries are one per channel carrying the track, the risk, the current version, the release moment and the size; its build-service entries are one per file, carrying a version and a kind. **A machine's build is a path with an answered version** (B10, B13). **So the match is a version comparison and can never be a build comparison**, which B13 states outright: *"A version number does not identify a build."*
- **The mark is therefore drawn per entry from the machine's answered versions, and it claims a version and nothing more.** Where a build on the machine answers the entry's current version, the entry is marked as that version being present. Where the machine holds a build of the same release line at a different version, the entry is marked with **the version that is present**, which is what the mockup's *4.5.9 installed* beside a 4.5.10 entry draws. Where neither, the entry is unmarked.
- **Nothing acts on the mark and that is what makes the imprecision safe.** Installing proceeds whatever the mark says, B8 never publishes over an existing folder, and the removal warning is composed from recorded **paths** rather than from versions (B14). **The mark is a reading for the user's eye and no decision anywhere reads it.**
- ~~**The list holds no daily build, and that is the ruling rather than an omission.** B6 records that blender.org's own builder publishes a machine-readable daily listing and that nothing in this document builds on it, because the Creator's ruling of 2026-08-12 fixes the list's source as the shop-front.~~ **Struck 2026-10-02: the list's unfinished builds come from that listing** ([B2](#51-the-list-and-the-fetch)), on the Creator's revision of that day. The mark is the same version comparison for them.

### 5.2 A build the user points at

**B7. Row 93 is `Server protocol and transfers` §7 and is cited, not re-solved.** Three clauses:

1. **The transfer** is the staging-and-publication contract in both directions: part-arrived bytes never sit at the final name, integrity and resume are measured against staged material, publication is the last step. That closes the row's own observation — a killed copy leaving a piece under the final name with no marker — and the one-space-three-tools breakage, because the staging path is synthesised from the transfer identity and no tool ever names it (S4, S5).
2. **A build that arrived short cannot look whole**: the digest is computed in Smoozy's own byte loop as the bytes pass and compared before publication (S22, S24). Only then is the archive unpacked.
3. **The unpack uses the one tool Blender's documentation names and nothing else** (Row 115). The failure is silent in both directions — the wrong tool reported success and the result would not start — so no exit status is read as the unpack having worked; the build is then asked its version under B1, which is owed anyway and is what catches a truncated or wrongly-unpacked archive, and the below-floor warning fires on the answer where it applies (the spec's own rule).

**B7a. The below-floor answer holds the publication for a human, and a decline ends the run completed with nothing published. Added 2026-08-25, per Row 489.** [Design](../Design/Server%20Setup.md) fires the warning between the version answer and the publication and offers *Install anyway* and a way out; B7 clause 3 and [R1](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of) item 7 place the moment, and what was stated nowhere is that the run can hold there for a user answer that is not an elevation, and what a decline leaves.

- **The warning can arise on this route only.** [B2](#51-the-list-and-the-fetch)'s list is floored at 4.5, and a build the ready action fetched must answer the expected version or fail the step ([R1](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of) item 7) — so no ending of [W1](#10-what-this-module-says-about-a-machine)'s is ever owed to the warning: W1's five are the ready action's, and the ready action never meets it.
- **The hold is [Y7](#33-the-worker-the-driving-and-the-stop)'s driving shape and no new mechanism.** On this route the version ask and the rename are driven as two commands: the ask is a machine-side read-back whose answer returns to the desktop, carrying no boundary of its own because its repetition is free ([Y5](#32-the-recorded-safe-boundaries)); the rename is not issued until the answer clears the floor or the user says install anyway. The worker waits at its last boundary as it does between any two steps, and **the refresh is not a step command and continues at its cadence while the question stands**, so the command-gap window does not end a run held on a human answer. The build sits in staging at no final name throughout (§7.1: publication last), which is what makes the decline possible at all.
- **A user who never answers leaves what a lost connection leaves.** The refresh stops when the sitting does, the window elapses, and the worker ends the run at its boundary with the kind's own *ended at boundary N, not completed*; staged material is kept and swept under [Section 11](#11-declarations)'s cleanup declaration. **A resumed run cannot slip past the question**: the version ask is owed before any rename (B7 clause 3), so [S4](#42-the-recovery-run)'s recovery re-runs the step after the marker, asks afresh, and the warning fires again on the fresh answer.
- ***Decline* is a step command, and the declined run completes rather than stopping.** The desktop commands the staged material's discard; the worker abandons it in S24's shape — never published, never repaired in place — and [B8](#53-where-a-build-goes)'s area never holds it. **The decline revises what the run contains**, the one act that changes [Y4](#32-the-recorded-safe-boundaries)'s composed list after run start: the publication step is struck and the discard recorded in its place with the reason, so [Y3](#32-the-recorded-safe-boundaries)'s boundary meaning holds against the revised list and a reading that meets the record later names what happened rather than meeting a list that quietly shrank. The run then proceeds to *done* — the final verification over what remains, the machine-level state (where the run was machine-changing, an unpack tool having been installed) cleared at *done* as ever, reconciliation run. **[S6](#4-interruption-resumption-and-the-safe-re-run)'s clearing set stays closed at two; this is its first clear, reached by the run finishing.** Work already at boundaries stays — a tool installed is installed.
- **The run's recorded outcome is *declined at the version answer* — a recorded ending, not a failure and not one of W1's five.** No step failed and no permission was refused; the record says the user was asked and said no, which is what Row 362's origin reading and any later panel name it as.
- **Not Row 113's stop.** A stop leaves a resume owed, and a resume of this run would re-ask and re-offer; a declined build must not be publishable by any later resumption, and the completed run with the discard recorded is what forbids it.
- **What a decline costs the user is the staged copy and nothing of their own**: the file on their computer is untouched (B7's source is the user's own disk), and installing after all costs the upload again. Checked against `Method`'s third escalation and declined, at [Section 14](#14-moments-that-reached-the-creator).

**B7b. What *Builds 10* adds to the unpacking route, and the two clauses it does not need to add. Added 2026-09-02, out of Open Threads 453–471; the answer is question 10 of `Preparation/Blender Research/Comparison/Builds.md`.** The item that routed this asked for one clause naming the `xz` pre-condition as a machine requirement rather than an unpack outcome, and one naming the library gap as separate from a successful unpack. **Both already exist and neither is rewritten here: [R1](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of) item 3 is the tooling step, which installs the tool *where absent* and unpacks with it and nothing else, and [R1](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of) item 4 is the libraries step, which reads the whole not-found list, installs the named packages and re-reads `ldd` clean.** What the answer supplies is evidence, and it moves three things.

- **The absent-tool failure is loud and total, in all four versions, and that half was not established.** On a deliberately bare rented Linux machine holding GNU tar 1.34 and no `xz`, **all four versions' archives refused at exit code 2 with nothing landing on disk** — tar could not run `xz` — and **naming the compression outright made no difference**. One further package fixed it, taking the machine from 253 installed packages to 254, after which all four opened with no message at all. Label: **observed**, one bare Linux machine, all four versions. **What it settles for R1 item 3:** a run that reaches the unpack on a machine without the tool fails at that step in the machine's own words and **leaves nothing behind**, so on this route there is no half-unpacked state for [Section 4](#4-interruption-resumption-and-the-safe-re-run)'s re-run rule to meet. The step was already ordered check-first; what is new is that the alternative is loud rather than partial.
- **No version's sources say it, which is why no reading could have closed this step.** Every version's manual names TAR as required and 7-zip as unsupported, and **names the archive's format nowhere**, so what TAR must in turn decompress is stated in no version. **R1 item 3's own history is the record of that**: it was parked on 2026-08-21 as a question this project could not answer with its documents open, and closed on 2026-08-22 by observation. The answer confirms the park was right and the documentation route was never going to close it.
- **The library list is a property of the image, and there are now two entries that disagree by count.** *Builds 10*'s bare machine could not satisfy **nine distinct libraries across ten lines, the same nine in every version**, and pulled in forty-two packages when nine were asked for; R1 item 4's stock `ubuntu:24.04` needed **ten packages** and took 17 seconds. **Both are observed and neither is doubted** — the images differ, which is precisely what R1 item 4 already says of its own entry, that the list is a property of the image and [E7](#9-elevation-the-refusal-and-the-password)'s requirement is that the mapping be fixed at build time rather than derived from a launch error. **So this is a second entry supporting that sentence rather than a correction of it**, and the whole-not-found-list rule is what makes the difference between images cost nothing. **Nothing here is a list to hard-code.**
- **And the 7-zip half is now settled version-wide, which [B7](#52-a-build-the-user-points-at) clause 3 anticipated and does not need widening for.** Two passes over each archive reported success and exited cleanly in all four versions; the runnable mark is gone and every symbolic link has become a plain file — the tar copies hold 71, 75, 77 and 81 links and the 7-zip copies hold none — and **putting the mark back by hand is not enough**, the program then failing on one of its own libraries. Clause 3's rule that no exit status is read as the unpack having worked is **confirmed** by this, and B1's version ask is the check that catches it, exactly as clause 3 says.

### 5.3 Where a build goes

**B8. Smoozy's own installs go into one Blender area on the same mount as the root, one folder per install, and publication is the last step.** The build is fetched and unpacked in staging and renamed whole into its folder — never over an existing folder, never over one a job is using (the copy-over-a-running-folder kill is the observed reason; deleting is harmless, copying over is not). The folder's name is the answered version plus the install's date and time, for a human reader — `blender-5.1.1-20261006-153212` *(changed 2026-10-06, the Creator's want, third notes on the test copy, note 4, ruled by the build runner; until then an identity of this document's issue plus the answered version; folders already on machines keep their names)*; nothing anywhere parses it back. **The date and time are the setup run's start, read from its record** — a recovery keeps the start of the run it took over, with its boundaries — so a recovery that takes over a run past *blender* composes the same name and finds the build where it was published *(added 2026-10-08, ruled by the build runner, written by the piece L builder)* — what a machine holds is read by looking ([B10](#54-the-search)), never from names. **And once an install run that published a build reaches *done*, by either route, [R1b](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of)'s comparison follows**: the version the published build answered, against the driver. Where a swap is owed, it is made by a run of its own, at once or once the server's work has ended. **A user's own build is compared only here**, because its version is not known until it answers ([B7](#52-a-build-the-user-points-at) clause 3). A build declined at the version answer ([B7a](#52-a-build-the-user-points-at)) was never published and is not compared. *Added 2026-10-11, later the same day, the Creator's ruling that the driver follows every Blender on the server.*

**B8a. Which of three ways a build got onto the machine is readable, and it is read from a record rather than from a name. Added 2026-08-21, per Row 362.** [Design](../Design/Server%20Setup.md) tags a row *your build*, and B1 and B8 between them close every route that reads a build's origin off a string.

- **The origin is not a property of the build and never can be.** B1 asks the build and the build answers a version and two marks; it does not answer where it came from. B8 forbids parsing the folder name back. B12 and Row 104 forbid remembering anything across connections. **So the reading has to come from a record of the act that placed it**, and one already exists.
- **A Blender install is an operation, its operation record is durable, and its subject is the published build.** `Rented-server runtime` [§13](Rented-server%20runtime.md#13-retention-on-the-machine) R1 already retains an operation record *"the same moment as their subject's machine-side record"* — **so naming the subject is the whole of this clause**: the record stands for as long as the build stands and ends when the build is removed.
- **The record names which of B5.1's two routes placed the build** — the published listing (B2 to B4), or a build the user pointed at on their own computer (B7) — **and nothing else about it.** No name, no channel, no provenance beyond the route.
- **The join is by executable path**, which is B13's own identifier and the only one this document trusts: each candidate the search returns (B10) is matched to an install operation record by the path it publishes. **Nothing is parsed and nothing is remembered on the desktop.**
- **So there are three answers and the design draws one tag.** *Smoozy placed it from the published listing* — no tag. *Smoozy placed it from a build the user pointed at* — the design's *your build*. **And *no install record names this path*, which is a build Smoozy did not place at all** — an apt or snap build the search finds (Fact 45), or one installed before this rule. **The third is real and common and the design has no tag for it**; what it is called, and whether it is called anything, is Design's.
- **A build removed and re-installed gets a fresh record**, because B8 never publishes over an existing folder and the removal ends the old subject.

**B9. The area's paths are composed here, and that was a recorded seam rather than a silence.** `Job files and outputs` N1 gave every machine path except the staging path to that document, and its 1.2 closed the root's areas at four. A Blender install is neither package state nor job material nor asset, and this document places it — so either N1 gained this document's Blender area as a second named exemption or the root gained a fifth sibling area. **The layout chosen here is a sibling under the root, widening that document's enumeration from four areas to five** (the hand-over then covers it and the mount is the chosen one); the enumeration is that document's and is not edited from here. **Closed 2026-08-22 at the Architecture seam close, both ways** — N1 names this document's Blender-area paths beside the staging path, and 1.2 L6 names five areas with the Blender area among them: [Section 13](#13-unresolved) item 8, and the report to the runner. **The Blender area is one of the four that sit directly under the root** and is untouched by L4a's placing of the package area on a mount of its own.

### 5.4 The search

**B10. Row 103: the search is a whole-disk file search for Blender's executable name, crossing filesystems, plus one asking-run per candidate under B1's discipline.** Fact 45's three traps are each closed by a rule: the version is parsed from the `Blender ` line and never by position; **deduplication is never by resolved path** — a snap's path resolves to the snap launcher, so the path-and-answer pair is the entry and the version each path reports is the discriminator; **the search is never restricted to one filesystem**, which silently loses a snap mounted from a loop device. The observed cost: 5.3 seconds on a 126 GB disk 12% full, plus a run per candidate.

**A build still in setup's staging folder is not a candidate. Added 2026-11-03 at Build, the runner's ruling, out of the Sesterce live pass; no auditor has seen this text.** The search found 5.2.3 inside `<Blender area>/staging-setup/<run>-unpacked/` while the setup that staged it stood failed, so the table offered it with *Remove* and the comparison of [R1b](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of) read its bar (**observed**). It is material of a run, published by [R1](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of) item 7 or never. **So every path inside that folder is dropped where the search's own area is dropped**, and the table, the `Render Settings` list and R1b's comparison all read what is left. **The bar of a build a run stages stands as item 5 has it**: the version a run was asked to install. **A recovery that was not asked for a version compares when its build is published**, R1b's moment for a build known only at its answer.

**The search does not descend into `/proc` or `/sys`. Added 2026-10-02**, out of the Creator's note 13 on 0.1.7 and the Sesterce pass of that day, where an ordinary login's search counted 2,679 folders it could not read, 2,578 of them under `/proc`. Both are the kernel's own information, not files: the Filesystem Hierarchy Standard 3.0 names `/proc` the *"Kernel and process information virtual filesystem"* (§6.1.5) and `/sys` the *"Kernel and system information virtual filesystem"* (§6.1.7) — **documented**, [Sources](Sources/Server%20Setup.sources.md). **This is not a restriction to one filesystem**: every other mount, the snap's loop device included, is still crossed.

**ADDED 2026-09-04, at a build sitting on rented machines rather than at a round, and no auditor of this document has seen it. One path is not a candidate: Smoozy's own Blender area, at the exact path [B9](#53-where-a-build-goes) composes.** The search is by name and the area is named `blender`, so on **every** machine this module has prepared the search finds it. **Observed in a test** on Hyperstack `Debian 12 Plain`, 2026-09-04: the whole-disk search returned five paths, **three of them directories**, and one of those was the area this module's own placement had made. Left in, it costs an asking-run that cannot answer and puts Smoozy's own storage folder in front of the user on [B11](#54-the-search)'s did-not-answer list, named as a Blender that would not say what it was.
