- **What is dropped is that one exact path and nothing else.** Not a prefix, not a rule about directories, and not anything else the search returns: **builds Smoozy placed live inside the area** and each is found at its own deeper path, which is the whole point of dropping the area's own path rather than the area.
- **This rests on a decision rather than on a fact about the world.** Smoozy composes that path, at [B9](#53-where-a-build-goes), and a build is never at it — so nothing outside Smoozy has to behave in any particular way for this to hold, and no reading is owed for it.
- **Whether any other path the search returns is a directory is not established and is not decided here.** A directory that is not this area is still a candidate: it is asked, it does not answer, and [B11](#54-the-search) names it — which is the honest outcome and is what the removal of the code's file-kind restriction on 2026-09-04 chose deliberately. **Narrowing further would need a reading of `find(1)` on a rental's own tool**, which this project has not done.
- **[B11](#54-the-search)'s two halves stay together.** A path that is never a candidate is on neither the listing nor the did-not-answer naming, which is the one place those two may agree to be silent; every path that *is* a candidate stays on both halves under B11's existing rule, and the refusal count is untouched.

**B11. What the search cannot do is kept in its answer, and one of it is said with the list.** A renamed binary defeats the file search and is found only by running every file, which is not done — a build the user renamed is invisible, and the answer records that the search is by name. A folder the login may not read is skipped and counted. **Both stay in the search's answer and in the diary, and neither is drawn. Creator-stated 2026-10-02**, note 13 on 0.1.7: *"All this should just go — it's unnecessary noise."* So no elevated retry is offered for the search either: the search is not a step refused, and the list is what this login can find. *(Expected, not established: a build in a folder this login cannot read is one this login could not run either, so the list stays what this login can render with.)* **A candidate killed at its time limit is not listed and is still named as one that did not answer**, because it is a thing that happened on this machine. ~~Where refusals occurred, the answer says the machine could not be searched completely, and the elevated retry is offered under [E2](#9-elevation-the-refusal-and-the-password)'s flow — a search run as root could not meet a refusal at all, which is why the count exists.~~

**B12. Row 104: the search runs on demand and its result is period-held, never durable.** It runs when Server Setup's own view is opened, when `Render Settings` asks for the version list, and before a removal is offered — never on connecting, because it costs a whole-disk pass and a run per build on a machine billed by the hour, and nothing needs it until one of those moments. **And again when an install or a removal on that server ends while a `Render Settings` page for it is open**, since the list it holds is no longer what the machine holds: the held list for that server is dropped and the open page asks again. *(Added 2026-10-19, out of the live pass on Vast for the thirteenth and fourteenth notes: 5.4 installed and the open page never offered it. No auditor has seen it.)* This is `State, identity and recovery` D4 applied, not an exception invented: the reading is taken fresh at the moment of use, carries its moment, and nothing durable is written from it.

**B12a. The folder the Blender table shows, and the link it carries into `File Browser`. Added 2026-08-23, per Row 440.** [Design](../Design/Server%20Setup.md) makes the table's middle column *"a link into File Browser at that folder"* and says of the screen that *"its folders link into File Browser"*. **The two are one promise**, because the per-build folder is the only machine path this screen composes.

- **The value is per build, and it comes from the reading that drew the row.** [B10](#54-the-search)'s search returns a path and an answered version per candidate, and [B13](#55-taking-a-blender-off) makes the executable's path the build's identifier — **so the folder is the directory containing that path, derived at the moment the table is drawn and never remembered** ([B12](#54-the-search), Row 104). **Never per version:** two builds answering one version have two paths and two folders, which is B13's rule reaching the column.
- **What crosses is the path alone, and no machine is chosen.** `File Browser` is the same server's other tab ([Design](../Design/File%20Browser.md), *Where it sits*), so the server row is the one whose screen this is; the path is the position the browser opens at, which is that document's own run-held browsing state (`File Browser` N1). **Nothing is picked and nothing comes back** — this is not the entry point `File Browser` [§12](File%20Browser.md#12-what-this-document-supplies-and-to-which-document) supplies to `Rendering`, whose callers are invoked with a machine and return a fresh pick.
- **Arriving at a path directly is already that document's ordinary case and is cited rather than re-solved.** `File Browser` P3 covers a browser that lands on a path: the stat answer names refusal or absence, and a refusal is walked component by component so the message names the component it arises at. **So this document composes the path and claims nothing about it** — the reading behind it is period-held, the folder may be gone or unreadable by the time the browser asks, and what is then said is `File Browser`'s answer under its own separation and not a statement of this document's.
- **On a build Smoozy did not place, the folder is the machine's own** ([B8a](#53-where-a-build-goes)'s third case): a snap's found path resolves to the snap launcher ([B10](#54-the-search)) and the snap mount is read-only squashfs ([B15](#55-taking-a-blender-off)), so the column can link at a folder nothing may be written into. **The link is offered on every row all the same** — withholding it for some builds would be this document deciding which folders the user may look at, which no spec grants it.

**B12b. Each Blender in the table carries a note where the machine's NVIDIA driver is below that Blender's OptiX bar: OptiX is off for this Blender on this server. Added 2026-10-11, the Creator's own addition to their rulings on OptiX and the driver.** The bar is `Rendering` L23a's, for the version the row's build answered ([B10](#54-the-search)). The driver's version is the reading `Servers` H11b holds for this connection. **Both are read, not remembered**, like the rest of the row ([B12](#54-the-search)). Where no NVIDIA driver answers, no row carries the note. The words are Design's, **and they follow what the row stops** (`Rendering` L23a, added 2026-11-02): from 5.3 the note says the driver is too old for Cycles on the card, not for OptiX. **On a server Smoozy can manage, the note stands while [R1b](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of)'s swap waits for the server's work to end, and it is the only thing the user sees of that wait.** It goes when the swap brings the driver up, because the next reading no longer finds the driver below the bar. *Added 2026-10-11, later the same day, the Creator's ruling that the driver follows every Blender on the server.* *Narrowed 2026-10-11, later still, the Creator's ruling that a job needing the newer driver is held back: the note is no longer the only thing shown. A job held for the swap carries its own telling on its queue entry ([R1c](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of); `Queue & History` H12). The comparison that draws this note is also the one [R1c](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of) reads at submission.*

### 5.5 Taking a Blender off

**B13. The matching rule Row 96 needs, and nobody else can supply: a Blender on the machine is identified by its executable's path; a job's recorded need carries that path beside the version.** A version number does not identify a build — two builds of one version share a settings folder and both answer the same version — so the submission payload records the picked build's path and its answered version, the manifest carries both, and the launch uses the path with `Rendering` L14's version confirmation guarding substitution. **L14 and `Queue & History` N10 both stand unchanged**: the version is on the manifest and the local record exactly as N10 says, and L14 still confirms the version by asking the binary. What is new is the path beside it, and `Job files and outputs` M1's field list now names it, with M1a stating its form — a seam recorded at [Section 13](#13-unresolved) item 9 and closed at the Architecture seam close, the same shape as `Rendering`'s two field-list seams.

**B14. The warning is composed from `Queue & History` §17's live set and the recorded paths, before anything is removed.** A job is live from its record's publication until its recorded outcome is written, held jobs included; the live set per machine is the job records with state *waiting*, *held* or *rendering*. The warning names every live job whose recorded build path **is the path of the build being removed, or sits under it where the removal is a folder deletion** — one predicate covering both of [B15](#55-taking-a-blender-off)'s routes, because a build the machine's own software manager installed has no folder of Smoozy's to be under and its recorded path may be a launcher rather than a folder at all (a snap's path resolves to the snap launcher, [B10](#54-the-search)). It then tells the truth about each. **On the folder-deletion route**: a job already rendering will finish — deleting the folder a render was running from was permitted at once and the render carried on to a normal end, all four versions, **observed on Linux and on a Mac; Windows untried and the deletion may be refused outright there**. **On the manager route that is not established and is not claimed**: what a removal performed by the machine's own software manager does to a render already running from that build was never observed, and this document does not carry one route's observation onto the other in the sentence the user reads before deciding — the warning says the job is rendering from the build being removed and that what becomes of a render in flight is not known for this route. Label: **not established**; [Section 13](#13-unresolved) item 17. On either route, a waiting job was submitted with that build and will have nothing to render with. What becomes of the waiting job is `Queue & History`'s. Smoozy never refuses and never removes quietly; the last-Blender case adds nothing on its own account (the spec's rule). *(Added 2026-10-16 by the build runner, from step 10c's owed piece 6: on the manager route the build being removed is the package, so the warning names every live job whose recorded path that same package holds — the snap's launcher and its own folder's executable alike — because the manager removes them together.)*

**B15. The removal itself is the plain deletion of the install's folder — except a build the machine's own software manager installed, which is removed through that manager.** A path under the system package areas is the manager's; deleting its files by hand leaves the manager's records lying about the machine, which is exactly the kind of stale reading Row 104 exists to forbid. **A path under the snap mount cannot be deleted by hand at all** — Canonical's own *System snap directory*, read 2026-08-20 at `https://snapcraft.io/docs/explanation/how-snaps-work/system-snap-directory/`, gives the mount as `squashfs (ro,nodev,relatime,x-gdu.hide)`, and a read-only mount refuses the deletion rather than leaving records behind. **So the rule is the same for both routes and the reason is not**, which is corrected here rather than left as one sentence covering two different mechanisms. The manager's removal is an elevated act and [E2](#9-elevation-the-refusal-and-the-password)'s flow applies. **The version-numbered settings folder is left behind in every case** — it is shared by every build of that version and holds add-ons the user may have placed (the Creator's never-install-over ruling reaches it in spirit), and a build removed leaving it behind is Blender's own documented behaviour.

---

## 6. Add-ons

**Parked, 2026-10-08: nothing in this section is built, and Smoozy installs no add-ons.** Label: **Creator-stated**. The Creator, word for word, in `Audits/Build/Step 10c/The Creator's rulings on add-ons and a file's scripts, 2026-10-08.md`: *"You know what? Let's ditch add-ons for now. You can put my own wording wherever the reasons for that go — I feel like this will create more problems than it will solve. Maybe someday when I have all the add-ons that fit the criteria of running at render time myself I will get back to it. But right now — we're not building it."* The spec parks its *Add-ons* section the same day. **A1 to A9 stand as the record of what would be built, and none of them is built**; the same holds wherever this document leans on them — Rows 97 to 101 and 309 in the table above, [Y1](#31-two-classes-of-setup-run)'s add-on install, [W3](#10-what-this-module-says-about-a-machine)'s add-on row, the told line *An add-on was installed*, [Section 12](#12-what-this-document-supplies-and-to-which-document)'s add-ons to `Rendering`, and [Section 13](#13-unresolved) items 3, 4, 11 and 24. **To be taken up again when the Creator holds the add-ons that run at render time.** **What stays:** `Render Settings` E5 still records the missing-engine announcement and that page says so, handing nothing here; and **A9a stands** — with no install, §12's read-back never stands for any build, so `Rendering` S3a's gate turns on the recorded reading's anchored identifier alone, and where none stands its item 27 already holds (`Render Settings` E2 fails the pick on the machine before the render instruction).

**Constraint:** Rows 97 to 101 and 309, and the Creator's ruling of 2026-08-12: the listing is taken as good enough and Smoozy never installs over what it cannot see.

**A1. Row 97: an add-on Smoozy fetches comes from a repository already on the fresh Blender's own list, and Smoozy adds no repository of its own.** The list sits in Blender's settings — the version-numbered folder shared by every build of that version, so writing to it is writing shared state — and a fresh Blender holds Blender's own extensions site among its three entries. Blender installs by name from a repository or straight from a file, never from a web address, and the installing command line forces the no-window mode anyway. **The machine's reach to the extensions site is established for the listing only — an actual extension package has never been fetched by this project** (Fact 54's named gap; [Section 13](#13-unresolved) item 3). A user's own copy takes the from-file route and touches no repository.

**A2. Row 98: what is packed is the add-on's directory as it sits in the user's own version-numbered settings folder, byte for byte, archived without rewriting anything, sent under §7's contract, and installed from file into the target build's own version folder.** The ordinary case is a machine of a different kind at the other end: the plain part travels and works, a compiled part built for the user's machine does not, and the failure arrives only when something uses it, that run reporting success too — so [A5](#6-add-ons)'s claim discipline is the whole honesty available. A compiled part built for the wrong Python is refused at install at a failing status — the 3.11/3.13 split between 4.5–5.0 and 5.1–5.2 — and is reported as the machine's own words behind a failed step. The observed transfer direction was the reverse of Smoozy's (packed on Linux, opened on a Mac), and the sources name that as not covered; carried at [Section 13](#13-unresolved) item 4.

**A3. Fact 56 is ANSWERED BY READING, 2026-09-02, in the direction this clause already stood — and the clause needs no change.** Smoozy does not ask for, read, or check a licence key. **What the reading settles:** the marketplace whose rules bind the paid Blender add-ons this question is about publishes that code products may be licensed as GPL or MIT and *"No other licensing options are currently permitted for code-based products"*, that both permit redistribution and modification, and that a compliant add-on's folder carries a `LICENSE` file beside the code with *"a license notice at the top of the file"* in every Python file. **So a copy carries licence text and a copyright notice, not an entitlement**, and neither permitted licence requires a seller-issued key for the copy to work. Label: **documented**; both pages, their addresses and their exact words are at [Sources](Sources/Server%20Setup.sources.md), Part 6. **The residue is narrower than the question was and is named rather than dropped:** neither licence forbids an add-on making a runtime activation check of its own, so **whether any individual add-on additionally holds a seller-issued key inside its folder is not established**, and this is one marketplace's rules on one date. **What that residue reaches is what the module *says* about licences — the spec's own statement — and nothing about this mechanism**, exactly as before; the silence is removed by the failure being the add-on's own, since an add-on that will not run unactivated says so itself. **The register's blind-rewording warning is honoured rather than spent**: nobody was asked anything, a publication was read, and the warning stands for the day the residue is put to a person. *(This read *Fact 56 stays unresolved … and it is not posed from here* until 2026-09-02, when the companion, `Facts.md` and this clause were left disagreeing after the reading landed in two of the three.)*

**A4. Row 99: the switch-on uses the route that reports, and the answer is trusted exactly as far as the research allows.** On the reporting route the act succeeds for a working add-on and raises for a broken one, naming the fault; the other route swallows the failure and the launch-time switch-on prints a complaint and still ends at success, so the quiet routes are never used. What is learned is *loadable at the moment it was switched on, in this sitting* — the list of switched-on add-ons records what was asked for rather than what loaded, asking Blender to look again reports nothing about damage, and nothing anywhere says beforehand whether the part a render needs will work.

**A5. Row 100: an install is judged by reading what the machine now holds, and the command's ending is read in neither direction.** One refusal ends at a successful status (a declared Blender-version mismatch, refused by name with nothing installed), one failure reports honestly (wrong-Python compiled part), and the same version declaration is ignored entirely for an older-style add-on whatever every version's manual says — so the manual is not the rule and the exit status is not the rule. **What is read back, and what counts as installed**: after the install, the target build is asked for its installed listing and its switched-on listing (with A4's switch-on made through the reporting route in the same sitting); *installed* means the add-on appears in the read-back listing **and** the reporting switch-on did not raise. What this module then claims is the spec's own sentence: installed and switched on, loadable at the moment it was switched on — and whether it works shows when the job renders. The read-back's known incompleteness — **no route is established to cover** switched-off older-style add-ons, and the listing says nothing about health — is carried, not papered over.

**Narrowed 2026-09-02, and the sentence was firmer than its evidence. This is a wording change and nothing in this document decides differently on it.** This clause and [A6](#6-add-ons) and [Section 13](#13-unresolved) item 4 each said the route *does not exist*. **What the research says is that none was established:** *Add-ons 10* lists as **not established** whether any route gives one complete list of everything installed including switched-off older-style add-ons, and its own summary is *"Smoozy can be sure something is there and can never be sure something is absent"* — a silence about what was found, not a demonstrated absence. **Every clause resting on it rests on the uncertainty and survives the narrowing**, which is why this is safe to change here alone. **The chain is not this document's to finish, and is routed rather than followed:** the flat wording lives in `Preparation/Specs/Server Setup.md`, is quoted verbatim in that spec's sources file, in `Rows/Server Setup.md` twice, and in this document's own [Sources](Sources/Server%20Setup.sources.md) — where it sits **inside a quotation of the spec and must not be edited into disagreement with the thing it quotes**. The spec moves first and the quotations re-sync with it.

**A6. Row 101: the folder written and read is the version-numbered settings folder of the build the job will render with, and the compare-before-install is against that build's own read-back listing.** Where the listing shows the add-on present, nothing is installed — *nothing is installed twice* is a promise about what the listing shows, per the ruling. Where the listing shows it absent, it is installed — and the residue is stated: an older-style switched-off copy is visible to no route this project established, so an install can land on a copy the listing could not show ([A5](#6-add-ons)'s narrowing of 2026-09-02); that is the bounded, ruled-on cost of taking the listing as good enough, and [Section 13](#13-unresolved) item 4 carries it. Patch releases share the folder (two numbers, not three); a removed build leaves the folder for the next one; **the Python changed between 5.0 and 5.1**, so an add-on with a compiled part installed for one is not usable by the other even where they share a folder.

**Where that failure surfaces is narrowed, and the narrowing takes the loud half away. Corrected 2026-08-31, out of the repaired Blender research.** This clause read *surfacing as A2's failing-status refusal at install time, or at use*. **Across the shared folder the install-time refusal cannot fire at all**: this clause's own compare-before-install finds the add-on already in the second build's read-back listing and installs nothing, so there is no install for the version check to refuse. **And nothing on record exercised that check across the change** — all five sharing tests used a plain-Python add-on with no bundled module, so the second version installed nothing in every one of them. Label: **observed in a test**. **So on the shared folder the failure surfaces at use and nowhere earlier**, which is [A5](#6-add-ons)'s claim discipline and never anything this module can promise away; A2's failing-status refusal stands for an install actually performed, which is a fresh install rather than the shared-folder case. [Section 13](#13-unresolved) item 24.

**A6a. Every install writes into a user repository and never into the folder a Blender runs from, and that condition is what the *no warning* row rests on. Added 2026-08-31, out of the repaired Blender research.** Every add-on install on record went into a **user repository** — the version-numbered settings folder [A6](#6-add-ons) already fixes as the target — and **nothing was ever written into the folder a running Blender was running from**, which is the one write recorded as killing a render on Linux ([B8](#53-where-a-build-goes)'s copy-over kill). Label: **observed in a test**. So *an add-on install disturbed no running render* is a result about writes into a user repository; it is not evidence about a write into a Blender's own folder, and it is never read as one.

- **The condition is already met by construction, and is stated here rather than left implicit.** [A1](#6-add-ons) installs by name from a repository or straight from a file, and [A6](#6-add-ons) fixes the folder as the target build's version-numbered settings folder. Neither route writes into a Blender's program folder.
- **One requirement follows and it is free.** Where anything this module runs sets where the **system** extensions repository lives, that location is pointed **outside every Blender's own folder**. The mitigation is the research's own and costs nothing. A system repository left inside a Blender's folder would put an install on the exact write that kills a render on Linux, and [W3](#10-what-this-module-says-about-a-machine)'s *no warning* row would then be wrong about the one step it says nothing about.
- **[W3](#10-what-this-module-says-about-a-machine)'s add-on row and [Y1](#31-two-classes-of-setup-run)'s additive class carry the condition rather than the bare result**, so neither reads as a statement that installing is harmless wherever it writes.

**A7. The offer's scope is the spec's**: per job, this machine, the Blender this job renders with; other builds need their own copies; a version changed after Pre-load leaves the list describing a version the job will not use, and Smoozy says so — the wider question is `Render Settings`', **settled at its Q12 (Row 142): a version change on that page discards the whole pre-load reading, this list with it** — *"The recorded reading for that (server, file) is discarded — every scene's values at once, the announced add-on case included, since an add-on belongs to one Blender rather than to the machine."* *(Corrected 2026-09-02 at the stage-close gate repair: this read* and stays parked *against a document that had settled it.)*

**A8. Row 111: what serialises a machine's setup is [Y9](#33-the-worker-the-driving-and-the-stop)'s one-run-at-a-time admission; what it cannot serialise is a Blender the user started, and a refusal from Blender's own add-on store lock is expected rather than read as a broken machine.** With one Blender holding the lock, an install fired from another refuses in under a second at a failing status naming the session that holds it. Smoozy does not parse the refusal for a cause — the step is reported failed with the machine's own words whole behind it (the same never-mined discipline as `Rented-server runtime` M24), the read-back of A5 says what the machine now holds, and the step is safe to run again ([S7](#43-why-each-step-is-safe-to-run-again)'s logic: an install re-run is the same act judged the same way). A Blender that is rendering does not hold the lock, which is why installs beside a render disturbed nothing; a running Blender does not pick a new add-on up by itself, and nothing here restarts one to make it.

**A9. Row 309: the one thing that crosses from Pre-load is a file naming an engine the chosen Blender lacks, and what follows the hand-off is Rows 97 to 101's machinery unchanged.** This module does none of the reading; the offer it composes from the hand-off is the same offer, and its claims are A5's.

**A9a. Where the user picks the engine themselves and the machine's Blender does not have it, the job is refused before it is sent — and the refusal is not performed here. Added 2026-09-02 at the stage-close gate repair**, against a live spec promise added 2026-09-01 that had no clause anywhere in this stage: *"Smoozy therefore refuses such a job before it starts rather than sending it, and says why."* **Why the promise exists rather than a fallback**: on a user-picked engine nothing falls back, three of the four Blender versions stop at a failure and **the fourth never stops — on a rented machine it runs until somebody destroys it, billing all the while**, so the case is an open bill rather than a wrong picture.

- **Who refuses.** The refusal is a gate on the submission, so it belongs to the document that composes and sends one — `Rendering`, ahead of that document's **S4** ordered unit (*"One scene-job's submission is an ordered unit"*), before the first step of it runs. **Nothing here composes, sends or refuses a job**, and writing the gate in this document would put a second arrangement beside the one that exists.
- **What this module supplies to it, and the whole of it.** [Section 12](#12-what-this-document-supplies-and-to-which-document)'s standing line to `Rendering` already carries it: *the add-ons installed and switched on through the machine's saved preferences per [A5](#6-add-ons)'s claim discipline and no more*. **So the fact the refusal turns on is A5's read-back** — the target build's own installed listing and switched-on listing, taken after the install, with A5's known incompleteness riding along.
- **The bar, and it is [B1](#5-the-blender-lifecycle)'s standing one.** What a machine holds is read by asking the build, never parsed from a folder name, a path or a channel. **So the refusal rests on A5's read-back of the engine's add-on and never on a build's engine list** — no list of engines a version is supposed to ship is consulted anywhere, because none is read anywhere in this document and inventing one here would be exactly the modelling B1 forbids.
- **What is owed elsewhere, recorded rather than written from here** ([Section 13](#13-unresolved) item 26): the gate clause itself, in `Rendering`, and a row quoting the spec sentence. **Neither is this document's to write**, and the row is in a file this document may not open.

---

## 7. The Telegram role's material, and its two entry points

**Constraint:** Row 91; `Telegram` §20's material list, which this document places and does not decide; `Rented-server runtime` §5.3 and W7 to W9; `Security and licence` §10.

**G1. The material is `Telegram` §20's list whole**: the token file, placed per `Security and licence` §10 — inside the authenticated SSH connection as part of this placement, never a command argument, a file owned by the supervisor's login permitting that login only, in the package state area, outside every job folder, never under a shared temporary area; the role configuration — the proved conversation identity, the machine's own identity and display label, and the machine-level reporting settings; and the durable enabled-or-disabled desired state, set through the ordinary command path (W8), with the supervisor doing every start and stop.

**G2. The Row 91 decision: material is placed by whichever entry point runs while Telegram is on, and is not placed in advance.** At first setup with Telegram on, placement is [R1](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of) step 8 of the ready action's run. Where Telegram is off at setup, nothing of it is placed. The dedicated entry point places the same material on a machine prepared earlier and sets the desired state to enabled, and does nothing else: no other setup step re-runs, the machine is not restarted, and a change of the desired state restarts nothing but the role worker (W9) — the no-restart promise kept by a mechanism already built, not by care here.

**G2a. Whether a connected server has the role, read for the pages that are not the card. Added 2026-11-03, the build runner's ruling, the second 5.3 live pass; no auditor has seen it.** Render Settings' Telegram line and Queue's *Telegram* group say the role is absent and must not slow the page they sit on, so neither takes the reading. **It is [Y6b](#32-the-recorded-safe-boundaries)'s own reading, asked of the machine off the drawing thread, at three moments and no others:** when a connection's tellings have been delivered (`Telegram` L3), which also covers the switch moved and a token replaced; when an act of Y9a's line has ended; and when a run that does not drive the line itself has ended, the add's own setup among them. **It is held per server for the run, nothing durable, and goes with the connection**, which Y6a's *the desktop stores none of it* allows. Only the newest reading asked lands, so one asked before a telling cannot stand over one asked after it. **Three answers: enabled, not enabled, and could not be read** — the last where the machine does not answer the request, its program is older than it, or no package area is recorded, and **then nothing is said**, on the page or in Queue: the page's choices stand as they always have. **The action is the dedicated entry point, by the same press the card makes**, so Y9a's line holds: it waits its turn behind a run. **It is not taken at connection by itself**: a connection is not the asking (`Telegram` spec, *A machine set up before Telegram was switched on*), and the material carries the token (G1). Whether the entry point is running or waiting is read from Y9a's line, not kept, and what it failed with is held beside the reading until the next press or the connection's end.

**G3. The dedicated entry point on a machine that holds no package places the package first and still restarts nothing.** The role worker is supervisor-hosted; without the runtime there is nothing to host it. The placement is the ordinary transaction (`Rented-server runtime` §2) through the bootstrap-and-repair request, which restarts no machine — on a container its registration step needs the elevated hook install (K11, K19), and [E2](#9-elevation-the-refusal-and-the-password)'s flow applies where the login must be asked for a password. The rest of the setup — the ready action's machine preparation — is not run.

**G4. Replacement deliveries reuse the same placement vehicle** (`Telegram` L4): the new token file replaces the old over the same route, and the told-record's bookkeeping is that document's.

---

## 8. The restart

**Constraint:** Rows 105 and 283 — the same promise in two specs, written once here — with Fact 51 answered yes and the escalation spent; Rows 106 to 109; Fact 57; Fact 80's cost spread.

**T1. The restart is `systemctl reboot`, issued over the connection Smoozy holds, as an elevated act under [E3](#9-elevation-the-refusal-and-the-password), only on the systemd kind, and only as part of a setup step that needs it** — which on the evidence is exactly one step, the driver's ([R1](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of) item 6). Observed: the connection reported `closed by remote host` and the same address and port answered again in about ten seconds with a new boot id and the disk intact (Fact 51). **Never through a provider's console or interface of any kind** — no provider exists anywhere in the app — and never on a container, where it would restart the container and repair nothing (Fact 48).

**Confirmed on a second seller, and the restart is now load-bearing rather than incidental — 2026-08-22.** [The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20%E2%80%94%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md): `sudo systemctl reboot` issued over the SSH connection already open **returned 0 and the connection dropped**; the box came back in **49 seconds**, and in **55** on a second restart later the same day. **The address was unchanged**, and **all three host keys were byte-identical** before and after. **What makes it load-bearing is [M1b](#1-the-machine-kind-and-what-a-setup-can-be-on-each)'s Debian route**, which cannot end in a working card query without it: the driver's module is built against a kernel the machine is not yet running. **So the whole-app boundary's carve-out is the difference between preparing a Debian machine and sending the user to their provider mid-setup**, rather than a theoretical allowance. **Not posed:** the same act on a container, where there is no service manager to ask — [R3](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of) already forbids it there, so nothing needs the answer.

**T2. The exception is exactly as narrow as the whole-app boundary states**: a restart a setup step needs, and nothing else. Smoozy never powers a machine on and never powers one down for good. Row 283 and Row 105 are this same promise and this section is both.

**T3. The user is not asked and is told** (the spec's rule): the machine-level state is durable before the command is issued ([Y4](#32-the-recorded-safe-boundaries)'s *restart issued* boundary; P4), so whatever happens next, the machine that comes back holds its queue back until the setup finishes.

**T4. Row 107: *this drop is mine* is a state Smoozy sets before the act, never a reading it takes after it.** A machine closing politely and a provider powering one off are detected in the same second, while a restart and a destruction look alike for the first few seconds — nothing in the moment distinguishes them (Fact 57; [The rented machine](The%20rented%20machine.md)). So before issuing the command, the desktop marks the server's connected period *restarting, caused by Smoozy*; while the mark stands, the dropped-connection repair machinery and the could-not-restore notification are suppressed and the server reads as restarting. The connected period itself, and the reconnect attempts, are `Servers`' — this document supplies the mark, the moment it is set (before the command leaves), and the moment it clears — **a closed set of two, and nothing else clears it: the machine answering ([T5](#8-the-restart)), or `Servers`' reconnect policy for a restart-marked period giving up.** **The user stopping the wait is not one of the two**: it ends the setup's waiting and nothing else ([T6](#8-the-restart)).

**T5. Row 106: when the machine answers again, the setup resumes by [S4](#42-the-recovery-run) item 2's recovery run from the *restart issued* boundary** — the reboot reaped the old worker, its group validates gone, and the recovery takes the state over and carries on at the check. Nothing resumes with nobody connected; the machine's only autonomous act while down and after return is holding everything back (P2, P4).

**T6. Row 108: the wait has no constant of this document's, and cannot have one.** Return cost was measured at 9 to 90 seconds across three machines, and a container recycle at about 90 — anything budgeting a fixed wait is wrong on most machines (K17). The structure is settled here; the figures are `Servers`': the wait ends one of three ways, and **only two of them clear the mark** ([T4](#8-the-restart)'s closed set of two). The machine answers (T5). Or **the user stops waiting**, which is Row 113's stop applied to the waiting and costs nothing the machine holds — and **clears nothing**: the mark stands, the machine-level state stands, `Servers`' reconnect policy for that marked period goes on running, and the setup is picked up on return; what ends is the user's waiting and nothing else. Or `Servers`' reconnect policy for a restart-marked period gives up, at which point the mark clears, the setup is reported cut off part-way per [S2](#41-what-a-part-finished-setup-reports), and Smoozy says only what it knows — the connection is down and could not be restored, with no claim to know why, because a machine still starting cannot be told from one that will never answer (the spec's own sentence). **What the server reads as while it waits is *restarting*** — supplied to `Servers` and Design as a state, not a wording. The attempt count and pause lengths are that document's to set against K17's constraint; this document's requirement on them is only that no figure is derived from any one machine's observed cost.

**T6a. What the restart panel may say about the render, bounded. Added 2026-08-21, per Row 359.** [Design](../Design/Server%20Setup.md) has the panel say *"The render that was running stopped, and restarts by itself."* **The stopping half is unconditional and is T7's.** The restarting half is not, and the boundary is exact rather than a hedge.

- **It is true for as long as the run is being driven**, which is for as long as the panel stands. Y2 keeps the interrupted job back on the durable state, and clearing that state at *done* runs reconciliation and re-runs it (P5) — so a run that goes on to finish delivers the sentence exactly.
- **The case that falsifies it is the user walking away.** Y7 drives the run from the desktop one step at a time, so a user who closes the panel and never reconnects leaves the run undriven, the state standing and the job never re-run. **That is not a silence**: the machine's own record is what S1 and S2 read on the next connection, and the user meets a part-finished setup naming what was reached and what to do. **The re-run is owed to a finished setup and the panel is where the setup is finished.**
- **[Section 13](#13-unresolved) item 16's case is worse and is not this one** — a dead setup group reading as live on a machine whose supervisor never restarts, where nothing defined here **cleared** the state at all **until X9a's wait closed it**. **The sentence is false there too**, and the route out is the reap `Rented-server runtime` X9a now requires.
- **So what this document supplies the panel is *the render that was running stopped, and is held to be re-run when the setup finishes*** — the stop as fact, the re-run as consequent. **The wording is Design's**; the drafted sentence states the consequent unconditionally, and that is recorded here rather than repaired in a document this stage does not own.

**T7. Row 109: what a restart stops, said per kind of work, with the unposed clause named.** A transfer in flight dies with the machine, and under §7's contract that is an *interruption*: the staged partial is kept off the final name, the record says interrupted, and it is resumed on the re-made connection (S26) — the observed 4.7 GB under a final name with nothing marking it was a raw tool's behaviour, and the contract exists so Smoozy's transfers cannot produce it. **A dependency check or any other reading is assumed to die with its worker, and that clause was never posed** — Fact 55's own *Not covered* line names it as the one still open. Its operation record has no outcome, and reconciliation — running after the setup clears the state — writes its disposition per §6. **A running render dies with the machine, and that is observed rather than assumed**: two real machine restarts with a render in flight, on machines restarted over the connection as root (Fact 55, *Six machines*). **Both interruption shapes are real and neither is aimable**: a restart landing in sampling — about 92% of a frame — leaves **nothing at the frame's path at all**, no partial and no zero-byte file; landing in the write — about 8% — leaves a partial **under the frame's real final name with nothing to mark it**, which settles that Blender does not write to a temporary name and rename. **A polite stop behaves identically to a hard kill at exit 143, and that is the signal a shutdown sends.** Both restarts landed in sampling despite the second being aimed deliberately at a write, because the shutdown latency varied from 9 seconds to 6.4 between runs minutes apart — **so the shape cannot be chosen even on purpose**, which is why nothing anywhere reads a file's presence as a frame's success. **Nothing announces the death**: the system log records only that it sent a termination signal to remaining processes, naming neither Blender nor the render nor its output. The observation is limited to **one output format and one picture size**. The earlier provider-console reboot is a different route and is not what this rests on, though it agrees — eleven whole frames, six zero-byte placeholders, and a render that did not come back. The **dependency-check clause** closes **unresolved** with what breaks named at [Section 13](#13-unresolved) item 2; what holds without it is that reconciliation's outcome does not depend on the manner of death (group gone → its own disposition), and `Queue & History` owns the re-run that follows.

---

## 9. Elevation, the refusal, and the password

**Constraint:** Row 117; `Security and licence` §4's invariant, its §13 rule 5 and that rule's named exception, and its §14 items 15 and 17; `Server protocol and transfers` §1.3's fixed route — the elevated-access password crosses on an elevating command's standard input and never as an argument.

**E1. Row 116's answer is [Section 1](#1-the-machine-kind-and-what-a-setup-can-be-on-each)'s table, and its weight is the spec's own**: putting a Blender on a machine and installing add-ons need no elevation at all — a self-contained folder, and files inside a version folder the login owns; the package placement needs none on a systemd machine and one root step on a container; **what leans on the machine's permission is making it ready**, and a machine that refuses may not be set up at all, said as [W1](#10-what-this-module-says-about-a-machine)'s refused statement.

**E2. Elevation is offered only at the moment a step is refused for lack of permission, never before, and the password's three lifetimes are Whole app's and are not retaken here** — one action, the connection, until cleared. What is owed here is the recognition, and it is E4's.

**E3. The route is the machine's own elevating command with the password on its standard input** — `sudo -S`, documented: *"Write the prompt to the standard error and read the password from the standard input instead of using the terminal device"* (`sudo(8)` 1.9.17p2, read 2026-08-19; [Sources](Sources/Server%20Setup.sources.md)) — because a command argument is readable by any other login on the machine (Fact 60), and §1.3 fixed this route before this document was written.

**E4. The validate-first order separates the authentication step's result from the requested operation's result — and that is all it separates.** The password is validated by the elevating command's validate form, which runs no command at all, before the operation is attempted. Documented, same manual: `-v, --validate` *"Update the user's cached credentials, authenticating the user if necessary. For the sudoers plugin, this extends the sudo timeout for another 5 minutes by default, but does not run a command."* Because it runs no command, a failed validate is never an operation's failure, and that much is the separable result `Security and licence` §14 item 15 records as owed by the route's owner.

**What a failed validate does not establish by its result alone is that the password was wrong; the text it carries does, for one of the three refusals, and for no other. Corrected 2026-08-31.** The same manual's EXIT VALUE names three causes of the same exit: *"If there is an authentication failure, a configuration/permission problem, or if the given command cannot be executed, sudo exits with a value of 1."* The validate-first order removes the third — there is no command — and leaves two. The second is not hypothetical: a login the machine's policy refuses to elevate at all fails the validate with a perfectly good password in hand, which is Fact 27's container answer in its own words — *"As the supervisor's login: elevation demands a password it does not have; switching to root fails authentication"*. **The two are not separated by the exit number. They are separated by the tool's words, and one of those words is read here: `Security and licence` §13 rule 5 carries a Creator-stated exception, dated 2026-08-25, permitting the elevation tool's wrong-password ending to be matched as an observed constant — and permitting nothing else. That rule is that document's; it is cited here and never restated as this document's own. Corrected 2026-08-31; this read *they are not separated here by reading the tool's words either*, which was written on 2026-08-22 and was overtaken by the ruling of 2026-08-25.**

**What the run showed.** [The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20%E2%80%94%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md), machine A, sudo 1.9.15p5, Ubuntu's stock sudoers, under a purpose-made non-root login: the three refusals **do** differ in their exact text, word for word — a wrong password ends `sudo: 1 incorrect password attempt`; no password at all gives `sudo: a password is required`; a login that is not an administrator gives `Sorry, user smoozynoadmin may not run sudo on a0e7a496ff47.` **All three exit 1.** So the exit number separates nothing, exactly as this clause said, and the words do — **and what may be done with them is `Security and licence` §13 rule 5's**: a tool's output is captured whole and never mined for a cause (and `Rented-server runtime` M24), **with one named exception, Creator-stated 2026-08-25, reaching this tool alone** — the wrong-password ending `sudo: 1 incorrect password attempt` is matched as an **exact observed constant**, which is matching rather than mining. **The clause used to say *nothing available here tells them apart*, and that is untrue as a statement of fact.** **Corrected again 2026-08-31**: it then read *the rule does not change; its ground does* and *Smoozy does not tell them apart because it may not, not because it cannot*, and the rule did change on 2026-08-25 — under its exception Smoozy does tell the wrong-password refusal apart. **What it still may not read is every other refusal.**

**And the observation is what the exception's line is drawn on.** The not-an-administrator message **names the host** — `… may not run sudo on a0e7a496ff47.` — so it is per-machine text, and any construction matching it as a constant would break on the next machine; the wrong-password ending carries nothing of the machine and is a constant. **So `Security and licence` §13 rule 5's exception reaches the second and never the first**, which is that rule's own bound, cited here and not set here. **Corrected 2026-08-31**: this paragraph read *the observation supplies the rule's own justification rather than being an exception to it*, which holds of the host-naming message alone and was overtaken for the other by the ruling of 2026-08-25. So:

- **a failed validate with a held password, whose captured text is the wrong-password ending** — `sudo: 1 incorrect password attempt`, matched as an exact constant and never as a pattern, under `Security and licence` §13 rule 5's Creator-stated exception — **is an authentication result separable from the requested operation's own**. `Security and licence` §4's invariant therefore fires rather than withholds, and its §2 item 3 erasure — *the password ceasing to work* — is performed on the remembered machine password. **The erase is that document's: its trigger is recognised here, and its order, its recorded debt and its sweep are §5.1's and are neither performed nor restated here.** The step is reported with the machine's own words behind it; no password is held afterwards, so a later refusal is the third case below and the offer is made again there. **Corrected 2026-08-31; this bullet said the password is kept, which was written on 2026-08-22 and overtaken by the ruling of 2026-08-25**;
- **a failed validate with a held password, whose captured text is anything else** — the not-an-administrator message, which names the host and is never matched; an unrecognised string; a release or machine kind whose wording was never observed — **is not established to be an authentication failure**, because rule 5's exception reaches the one constant and no further. `Security and licence` §4's invariant governs in its withholding direction: *"Where the two cannot be separated, the password is **not** forgotten."* **So the password is kept**, the step is reported **refused** with the machine's own words behind it, and the case is [W1](#10-what-this-module-says-about-a-machine)'s **Refused** ending — the same ending [M4](#1-the-machine-kind-and-what-a-setup-can-be-on-each)'s no-route machine reaches. What that costs is named at [Section 13](#13-unresolved) item 6, and `Security and licence` §14 items 15 and 17 carry the same limit from that side;
- **a failed operation after a successful validate** is a failed operation: reported as such, with the machine's words behind it, and it forgets nothing — a full disk, Blender's add-on lock, a wrong-Python part and a real fault all land here, and none of them touches the password;
- **a refusal with no password held** — the non-interactive probe failing (M3) — is the moment the offer is made and the ask happens, at that moment and not before.

Two limits, both carried: the validate's caching (*"Not all security policies support cached credentials"*) is not relied on — where the cache does not hold, each elevated command carries the password on standard input again, and the separation stands because the validate's own result is what classifies; and **the validate form has now been run on a rental — 2026-08-22, and it behaves as documented.** Under a purpose-made non-root login in group `sudo`, `sudo -S -v` with the right password prompted `[sudo] password for smoozytest:` and **exited 0 with no command run** ([The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20%E2%80%94%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md)). **This clause used to read *the validate form has never been run on a rental — its behaviour there is documented rather than observed*, and that is now spent.**

**One rider, and it is a per-machine reading rather than a constant.** The credential cache on that image is **15 minutes** (`sudo -V`: *Authentication timestamp timeout: 15.0 minutes*), and a `sudo -n id -u` inside the window returned `0`, so the caching is real there. **That is not a divergence from documentation, and it used to be written as one — corrected 2026-08-23 by reading the rental's own release.** `sudo(8)` at **1.9.15p5**, the version machine A carried, as Ubuntu publishes it at `https://manpages.ubuntu.com/manpages/noble/man8/sudo.8.html`, read 2026-08-23: `-v, --validate` — *"Update the user's cached credentials, authenticating the user if necessary. For the sudoers plugin, this extends the sudo timeout for another 15 minutes by default, but does not run a command."* **So the observed figure is exactly its own version's documented default**, and the five-minute figure quoted above is the Creator's Mac's page at 1.9.17p2. This clause used to read *against the manual's documented five-minute default*. **The rule is unchanged and better founded**: the window is read per machine and never assumed from any one page's figure, because two of the tool's own pages give two different defaults. **The EXIT VALUE sentence E4 rests on is word for word the same at 1.9.15p5**, so the separation above is untouched. Nothing above leans on it: the caching is already not relied on, and each elevated command carries the password again where the cache does not hold. [Section 13](#13-unresolved) item 6 records what is left.

**E5. The middle lifetime spans a machine reboot, deliberately.** A password kept *for as long as the connection lasts* is keyed to the connected period, and the connected period survives a restart Smoozy caused (Row 107) — so the password survives the reboot the setup itself performed. That is the spec's own construction meeting itself, not a new cost; [Section 14](#14-moments-that-reached-the-creator) records the check. A connection that ends any other way takes every process-memory password with it (`Security and licence` §5.2).

**E6. What is never done**: no retry loop on an authentication (rate limiting corrupts the diagnosis — §4), no password in an argument or environment value ever, no reading of the machine's settings into any log — the one box on record held the provider's own secrets in plain text among them ([The rented machine](The%20rented%20machine.md)) — and no second route beside the elevating command.

**E7. Every elevated command this document issues is checked against [Server protocol and transfers](Server%20protocol%20and%20transfers.md#11-what-the-surface-is-made-of-and-it-is-two-mechanisms-rather-than-one) 1.1's admissibility rule, and the declaration is made once, here.** The rule holds in either phase — no value of user, provider or machine origin is ever interpolated into a command string — so each step's values ride one of the rule's carriages or are fixed at build time, per step:

- **the hand-over of the chosen root, and of the package area's second mount where [R7](#23-what-the-placement-is-given) has one** — the only machine-origin paths an elevated step of this document must name — the planted-link carriage, one link per mount, declared with its link-treatment citation at [R7a](#23-what-the-placement-is-given);
- **the owner that same hand-over's `chown` must name — the login the mount is handed to, which is the supervisor's and is the connection's own ([M4](#1-the-machine-kind-and-what-a-setup-can-be-on-each)), and that login's own group where the command form names one — the fourth carriage, machine self-derivation. Added 2026-09-04; this enumeration declared the hand-over's path and stopped there, while [R7](#23-what-the-placement-is-given)'s act names an owner as well.** The value is not a path and it is not the receiving program's standard input, so none of the first three carriages reaches it: it is an argument the elevating command must consume, and 1.1's fourth is written for exactly that class — a non-path value about the connection's own login that the invoking process's own credentials answer. **The command string stays constant and no login name crosses from Smoozy at all**: the value originates on the machine, becomes an argument there, and is never carried from a reading Smoozy took earlier. The carriage's citations — the login shell behind the string, the substitution's own semantics, and the derivation itself — are 1.1's and are not restated here; they cover the login's group beside the login. **`Rented-server runtime` K5/K6's lingering grant and read-back are the same carriage's standing instance**, so nothing is invented for this step. **The carriage reaches no further than the credentials the invoking process itself carries** — never a package, path or other name read off the machine and echoed back, which stays inadmissible ([Section 13](#13-unresolved) item 21). **A mangled derivation names no login the machine holds and the step fails with the machine's own words behind it**, and [R7](#23-what-the-placement-is-given)'s real write of at least one byte at the exact target path is what proves the ownership landed — the command's exit is never that evidence, for the owner half exactly as [R7a](#23-what-the-placement-is-given) already requires for the path half;
- **the container boot hook (K11, K19)** — the destination path and the mode change are constant, per K10's fixed provider path; the hook's content crosses the subsystem to a Smoozy-synthesised staging token, and the elevated command names the token and the fixed destination — both admissible classes, no link consumed;
- **the restart ([T1](#8-the-restart)), the driver route of [R1](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of) item 5, the package-system repair ([S7](#43-why-each-step-is-safe-to-run-again)) and the lock-timeout setting ([S7b](#43-why-each-step-is-safe-to-run-again))** — constant strings entire: fixed program names, fixed options, fixed values — and [R1](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of) item 1's readings the same, elevated or not;
- **the driver support binaries of [R1](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of) item 5's Ubuntu arm — the fifth carriage, and it is new. Added 2026-09-23, out of step 10c's fifth pass.** The publication's own additional-components install carries a driver branch number, and which branch a machine gets is the detection route's choice rather than this document's, which item 5 forbids this document making. **The command string is constant entire and the branch never crosses Smoozy at all**: the branch of the driver package the install left is read on the machine, inside a double-quoted command substitution the login shell expands there, and its answer becomes one argument. That is `Server protocol and transfers` 1.1's fifth carriage, whose shell, substitution and one-word-quoting citations are the fourth's own and are not restated here. **The fourth carriage's bound is unchanged and so is [Section 13](#13-unresolved) item 21's**: a value Smoozy read off the machine and echoed back into a command string stays inadmissible, and nothing here does that — Smoozy neither reads the branch nor writes it. **A read that finds no branch composes no package name**, apt answers in its own words that there is no such package, and the step ends there; *(Not established since 2026-11-03: `apt-get(8)` reads a name ending in a hyphen as a removal, and on the Sesterce pass the route read *done* with no driver package on the machine. Which of its acts printed *0 upgraded* is not established. [R1](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of) item 5's reading after the route is what now holds, whichever it was.)* **from 2026-10-03 the same carriage also takes R1 item 5's fifth act, the OptiX library's package for the same branch, composed the same way and bounded the same way; and from 2026-10-11, R1 item 5's swap up to the OptiX bar, for its two readings — the old branch, read from the driver's own version query and passed on only where it is exactly three digits, and the running kernel's release, for its headers package — each read on the machine and quoted as one word, so nothing crosses from Smoozy; and from 2026-11-03, R1 item 5's finishing of a removal that stopped part-way, for the branches and the flavour it reads with `dpkg-query` from the kernel half left behind and for the running kernel's release, read on the machine in the same way, so nothing crosses from Smoozy here either;**
- **the tooling install ([R1](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of) item 3)** — the one unpack tool Blender's documentation names, a constant package name;
- **the libraries install ([R1](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of) item 4)** — the machine's `ldd` list **selects, and never composes, the command**: the packages named are drawn from a fixed build-time mapping of library names to package names, so every byte of the command string is fixed at build time and no machine-read name is echoed into it. A library the mapping does not name fails the step honestly, with the machine's own words behind it, rather than riding the command string;
- **the manager-route removal ([B15](#55-taking-a-blender-off))** — the constant package name the machine's manager holds Blender under, the same fixed names [B2](#51-the-list-and-the-fetch) already reads the store by; a managed build under any other name has no admissible carriage for its removal and is reported as one this module cannot remove ([Section 13](#13-unresolved) item 21);
- ~~**the elevated search retry ([B11](#54-the-search))** — the same constant search command as the unprivileged pass: fixed name, fixed root;~~ **no longer offered, 2026-10-02 ([B11](#54-the-search))**;
- **the password** — the elevating command's standard input, read as data, which is 1.1's standing instance of that carriage (E3).

The machine-side steps of [Y7](#33-the-worker-the-driving-and-the-stop) — the fetch, unpack, `ldd` reads, publication and read-backs — are the setup worker's and touch no command string; file placement and every read-back ride the file-transfer subsystem, whose paths are protocol fields.

---

## 9b. The user-facing fallback re-run

**Constraint:** [The shape](The%20shape.md#each-rented-server) — *"Server Setup's user-facing re-run is the fallback if automatic repair fails"*; `Server protocol and transfers` §5 (B7: nothing about the request is automatic in that document); `Desktop app and delivery` §12 V10; `Rented-server runtime` §16 item 12's named condition.

**F1. The fallback re-run is a user-triggered action on a connected server that inspects and then acts, entirely on the pre-package surface, so it works on a machine whose supervisor is dead.** In order:

1. **Package inspection** (`Server protocol and transfers` §5, B4): the active-package pointer's version, the boot-registration record's content, the phase, the installation identity and the adapter's declared request set — compared by content, never presence. **An installed package speaking the immediately previous supported server protocol is not *different* for this step and does not trigger repair merely because it is previous.** *(Narrowed 2026-09-28, outside any round; no auditor of this document has seen it.)* **Replacing a working program because its release is below the application's own is `Desktop app and delivery` V11's**, which runs at every connection and before this re-run could be pressed; this step adds nothing to it and does not repeat it. The desktop selects `Server protocol and transfers`' previous-protocol adapter and retains that adapter's control route for inspecting, stopping, releasing and collecting its live or held work. A protocol outside that document's compatibility window is unavailable and is reported as such; this document does not widen the window.
2. **Where the package is absent, outside the compatibility window, fails release-provenance verification, or its phase is not terminal** — **and never where its release is above the application's own, which `Desktop app and delivery` V14 forbids (added 2026-09-28)** — the bootstrap-and-repair request is sent, user-triggered here exactly as B7 requires, under the same commit and rollback rules as the automatic route (`Rented-server runtime` §2). Before any phase changes it acquires **G1a's or G1b's** exclusive package claim — G1a's kernel-held lock where a machine-side program is performing the transaction, G1b's exclusive create where the desktop is (corrected 2026-08-22). Its requested version resolves through G26 to G31's signed manifest, exact target tuple and digest rather than through a version label. A *deferred* answer — live or held work blocking the commit — is reported as the ordinary result it is (B6), not a failure; the previous-protocol control route remains available while that work stands.
3. **Where the package is present, confirmed, and the supervisor does not answer** — the re-run issues **the plain start of the registered service over the command channel**: the user-unit start command on a systemd machine; on a container the keeper heals by itself the moment its own obstacle clears and no start is owed. This is the step `Rented-server runtime` §16 item 12 names as this document's and deliberately does not answer — a full state mount stops the supervisor from starting, and after space is freed the systemd unit did not recover merely because room returned; the machine recovers completely on one plain start command. **Re-checked 2026-08-22, and the step is unchanged.** What produced the non-recovery is the start limiter, which is a per-unit setting the placement itself writes — `systemd.unit(5)` gives `StartLimitIntervalSec=` as one that *"may be set to 0 to disable any kind of rate limiting"* — so a placement written that way removes the condition this step exists to reach a machine in. **Whether the placement is written that way is `Rented-server runtime`'s and is not settled there**, and the observed unit was left at its defaults, so this step is not withdrawn on the strength of a setting nobody has chosen. The disk-full negative is already ruled against all three escalations and is not re-escalated; what is added here is the one command, in the one condition, from the one route that reaches a machine in it. Freeing the space is the user's, through Smoozy's ordinary file operations, which stay available while the supervisor is unavailable (the shape's own sentence).
4. **Where the supervisor answers** — nothing is owed; the re-run reports the machine healthy and that nothing was owed, which is what the user was promised. **What step 1 read stays inside the module**: the active-package pointer's version, the boot-registration record's content, the phase, the installation identity and the adapter's declared request set are this module's own bookkeeping, and no spec grants the user a view of any of them.

**F2. The fits, stated for the record.** The re-run sends exactly §5's request and adds no variant to it; the automatic trigger stays `Desktop app and delivery`'s and V10's no-second-automatic-route rule is untouched — this route is user-facing only. Item 12's *what performs that command* is answered by F1 step 3; `Desktop app and delivery` §22 item 15 narrows but stands, because the **automatic** path still cannot start a supervisor whose package inspects healthy — its trigger sends a request whose phase is *verified* and which therefore acts on nothing (G17). Whether that document routes its trigger through the same start step is its own one-line decision and is reported to the runner rather than written from here.

**F3. The fallback has no package-transaction exception.** Two application instances, an automatic repair and this user action all contend for **the same claim at the same path — `Rented-server runtime` G1a's kernel-held lock where a machine-side program is performing the transaction, G1b's desktop-side exclusive create where none is (corrected 2026-08-22; this used to name G1a alone, and the re-run reaches machines with no program on them).** None is privileged over another and none terminalises a live owner's phase. After a genuinely abandoned owner releases the claim, whichever route acquires it re-reads and finishes the recorded attempt under its recorded identity before beginning its own. **Where the claim is G1b's, *live* reads one degree weaker and G1b says so** — an unacquirable claim naming another installation may be live or abandoned, which is `Rented-server runtime` §16 item 23's cost and not a licence to break the claim. **A waiter giving up changes no machine state.**

---

## 10. What this module says about a machine

**Constraint:** Rows 89, 295 and 308 — all three about what is said rather than what is done.

**W1. The ready action ends in one of five statements, and they are distinguishable by construction, not by wording:**

- **Ready** — [R4](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of)'s claim, no more.
- **Nothing to prepare** — every check that had a subject passed before any act was needed: where there was a Blender to read against, its library chain resolves; the driver answers; nothing was installed. Said plainly and not presented as a fault — the option had nothing to do, which is not the same as failing. **Where the machine held no Blender and the run fetched none, the library half was not read at all, and the statement says so rather than claiming a check that never ran** ([R1](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of) step 4, [R4](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of)): whether a build's libraries resolve is known when a build arrives, which is when that check is reached. That rented machines arrive arranged this way is no longer an assumption: **the locked container with its drivers already in place is observed** (Fact 48). **Narrowed 2026-08-23; this used to add *and is the commonest rental kind*, which is one seller's claim about its own instances stated as a property of rentals.** Fact 48's *"Instances are almost always Docker containers; a small subset are virtual machines"* is **Vast.ai's own documentation about Vast.ai**, and the three-seller batch of 2026-08-22 delivered **virtual machines on the other two sellers** ([The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20%E2%80%94%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md)). **Nothing here turns on which kind is commoner**: every rule in this document is written per kind ([M2](#1-the-machine-kind-and-what-a-setup-can-be-on-each)) off a reading taken at every placement and never carried forward (K2).
- **Not a kind Smoozy prepares** — [M1a](#1-the-machine-kind-and-what-a-setup-can-be-on-each)'s family reading placed the machine outside Ubuntu and Debian, or could not place it at all. Added 2026-08-22 with the Creator's ruling. **It comes before every other ending because it is reached before any step is composed**: nothing was installed, nothing was touched, no setup-in-progress state was ever set, and the machine is left exactly as found. **It is not *Refused* and not *Could not be made ready***, and the difference is real rather than a shade of wording — those two describe a machine that was worked on and would not take it, while this one was never worked on. Naming what was read where anything was read is what keeps it from reading as a fault: the user rented a kind this module does not prepare, which the spec told them before they rented. Where the machine would not say which family it is, the same ending is used and says that instead; **an unreadable answer is never defaulted to a supported family**.
- **Refused** — a step was refused for lack of permission and the user declined elevation, or the machine offers no route ([M4](#1-the-machine-kind-and-what-a-setup-can-be-on-each)), or a failed validate whose text is not the wrong-password constant, which is not established to be a wrong password and so keeps the password ([E4](#9-elevation-the-refusal-and-the-password)): the machine may not be set up at all, the spec's own narrowing, named with the step that was refused. **Corrected 2026-08-31; the third clause read *an elevation the login is refused could not be told from a wrong password*, which the exception `Security and licence` §13 rule 5 took on 2026-08-25 narrows to the unmatched refusals.**
- **Could not be made ready** — the action ran and **a step of it failed and could not be got past**, the card never answering being one case of it rather than the whole of it (the terminal container fault; the Secure Boot case of [R9](#25-secure-boot-and-the-machine-smoozy-cannot-make-ready)), named with the machine's own words behind it, which [S2a](#41-what-a-part-finished-setup-reports) already records on the machine. **Widened 2026-09-04, out of the round-1 code audit of this module's build; it read *the action ran and the card never answered*.** A step can fail for none of the other four reasons and did not reach this one: the libraries step can meet libraries the fixed mapping does not name (R1 item 4), the tooling install can be refused for a full disk, the package system can report itself busy with its own updates ([S7b](#43-why-each-step-is-safe-to-run-again)), the fetch can fail its digest ([B4](#51-the-list-and-the-fetch)), and the Debian components step can fail and leave the driver unreachable (M1b) — and `Preparation/Specs/Server Setup.md` promises under *When a setup fails, or is cut off* that *"A step that fails is reported here, and can be run again from here."* **No sixth ending is added**: a step that failed is a machine that was worked on and would not take it, which is what this ending already means, and the five are what the Design draws.

**The family ending outranks all four, and by construction rather than by rule**: it is decided from a reading taken before the sequence is composed, so no step has run to refuse anything or to leave a card unanswered. **The precedence between the last two, because one machine can satisfy both.** Fact 48 makes a container's non-answering card terminal for that server and Fact 27 makes nothing open at all from a non-root login, so a container can be refused and unmakeable at once. **Where the machine kind closes the driver route by construction and the card does not answer, the ending is *Could not be made ready*, whatever else was refused** — [R3](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of) settles that a container's driver routes are closed and are never probed by trial, so no elevation the user could grant would change the answer, and putting an elevation offer in front of them would be an ask no step needs, which [E2](#9-elevation-the-refusal-and-the-password) forbids. **Where the card answers and only a permission refusal stopped the action, the ending is *Refused*** and the elevation offer stands. That test is what *distinguishable by construction* means for this pair.

**W2. What every one of those statements withholds, on the spec's own bound: whether the machine renders on its cards.** An ordinary job will not settle it — with the driver missing or wrong, every version rendered on the processor and reported success — so the statement is: whether the cards work will be known once a job has run on them, and until then nobody can say.

**W3. Row 295: the warning is a property of the step, the table is closed, and silence where a step cannot disturb is enforced by the table rather than by judgment.**

| Step | Warns? | What is said about the work |
|---|---|---|
| ready action containing driver work or a restart | **yes** | the live jobs named from `Queue & History` §17's live set; a running render is taken down by the restart — **observed**, two real machine restarts with a render in flight (Fact 55) — and re-run after the setup finishes (Row 110); a transfer is interrupted and carried on ([T7](#8-the-restart)). **The OptiX swap is not driver work for this row**: it is never made over live work ([R1b](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of)) |
| the driver brought up to the OptiX bar, in a ready action or a run of its own ([R1b](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of)) | no | made only where nothing is live and no transfer is under way, and read again once the machine-level state stands, so there is no work for it to disturb. *Row added 2026-10-11, later the same day, the Creator's ruling that the driver follows every Blender on the server.* *A job held for the swap ([R1c](#21-what-the-action-is-made-of)) is not live for this purpose and is not disturbed either: it has not started, and a hold survives every restart (`Rented-server runtime` U24). Added 2026-10-11, later still* |
| Blender install alongside, on a machine that already holds the unpack tool | no | observed harmless, every frame written — and the observation is of an unpack and a rename beside a running render, which is exactly what this row covers |
| Blender install alongside, where the unpack tool must be installed | **yes** | the step performs an elevated package install, which the *no* row's observation never covered; the run is machine-changing ([Y1](#31-two-classes-of-setup-run)) and the live jobs are named from `Queue & History` §17's live set |
| add-on install | no | observed harmless, frames identical picture against picture; the rate limit of the observation carried, **and its write-location condition** — every install observed went into a user repository and none into the folder a running Blender was running from ([A6a](#6-add-ons)) |
| Blender removal a live job's build matches | **yes** | [B14](#55-taking-a-blender-off)'s warning — rendering finishes on the folder-deletion route (**not established** for the manager route, [Section 13](#13-unresolved) item 17), waiting has nothing to render with |
| Telegram material, desired-state change | no | restarts nothing but the role worker (W9) |
| package placement, repair, replacement | no warning of this document's | the whole commit is blocked while live or held work depends on the runtime and the caller is told it is deferred (G21, B6); **where it is the update of an older program, the server says it waits, and the one way past it is `Desktop app and delivery` V15's stop-and-clear, which carries `Servers` W19a's statement before it acts** (added 2026-09-28) |

A step whose effect is settled later slots into the table by the same test — could it disturb work — rather than changing the rule (the spec's own construction). Warning where nothing can be disturbed is what trains the user to click through the one that matters, so the *no* rows are as binding as the *yes* rows.

**W3a. The reading behind the three lines is over every finished job the machine still holds, not over one.
